November Click Spikes In Google Search Console Are Bot Traffic – How To Filter Your Reports via @sejournal, @marie_haynes

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

That sudden click spike in Search Console isn’t real traffic. Here’s what my data shows, what’s causing it, and how to filter it out.

Earlier this week, I saw Barry Schwartz’s post on X (and his write-up on Search Engine Roundtable) sharing a screenshot from Joy Hawkins. Joy noticed that several sites were seeing sudden, massive vertical spikes in Google Search Console clicks while impressions stayed flat, and Barry asked a great follow-up question: “Anyone else seeing this? If so, how are you filtering the report…”

Screenshot of Barry Schwartz and Joy Hawkins’ post on X highlighting the sudden click spike in Google Search Console. (Image Credit: Marie Haynes)

When I saw Barry’s tweet, I immediately thought this would be the ultimate real-world test for Marie’s Brain – my soon-to-be-launched agentic SEO and AI search analysis system.

Over the past several months, I have been building Marie’s Brain to combine my 15+ years of research on Google’s ranking systems with live, programmatic access to the Search Console and analytics data of the sites I monitor. Instead of manually clicking through Search Console property by property to see if any of my clients were affected, I asked my system to scan across all of the sites I monitor, check the unfinalized fresh data through Nov. 2, 2026, and flag any property showing a sudden anomaly in clicks.

Within seconds, Marie’s Brain surfaced the exact same vertical spike across multiple sites in completely different industries – and pulled the raw page, query, device, and country dimensions so I could see what was actually happening under the hood.

Just like Glenn Gabe and Joy Hawkins mentioned in Barry’s Search Engine Roundtable post, the majority of properties I monitor showed completely normal traffic. However, while some SEOs saw the wave begin over the weekend (Oct. 31 to Nov. 1), a distinct subset of sites I monitor experienced a dramatic vertical spike on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026 that mirrors Joy’s screenshot:

Six-month daily Google Search Console clicks (Search type: Web, including fresh unfinalized data through Nov. 2, 2026) across three affected properties I monitor. Image Credit: Marie Haynes

Here is how those three anonymized properties behaved on Nov. 2, 2026 compared to their prior seven-day baseline (Oct. 26 – Nov. 1, 2026):

When I had my system drill into the raw API rows for Nov. 2, two unmistakable patterns emerged across the affected sites:

Visual summary of why total impressions look flat while automated deep-SERP clicks create a vertical spike in Google Search Console. Image Credit: Marie Haynes

Normally, organic clicks in Search Console are heavily concentrated in the top 3 to 5 results on Page 1. On Nov. 2, click-through rate (CTR) exploded on pages ranking between Position 11 and Position 55 (Pages 2 through 6 of Google):

Why did total impressions stay flat if every click also had an impression? It comes down to simple scale. When a site normally gets 18,000 impressions and 50 clicks per day (like Site A), adding 275 automated impressions is only a 1.5% bump on the impression line – completely invisible on a chart! Adding 275 clicks to a 50-click baseline, on the other hand, creates a massive 6.5x vertical spike.

On Site C (a technical troubleshooting Q&A site), a single long-tail troubleshooting page that normally gets zero to one click per day (and never received more than seven clicks in a single day across the entire previous six months) suddenly jumped to 13 clicks on Nov. 1 and 40 clicks on Nov. 2.

While the spikes on Sites A and B came from U.S. searches, all 40 clicks on this Site C page came from Canada. From Oct. 28 to Oct.r 31, that page only had four to seven impressions per day in Canada (and 0 clicks) while ranking on Page 2. Then on Nov. 2, Canadian impressions on that page jumped by ~45 (to 50 impressions), and Canadian clicks jumped from 0 to 40 clicks – meaning almost every single one of those new impressions was clicked (an 80.0% CTR at average position 14.6 on Page 2, while U.S. clicks on that page stayed at 0)!

When I inspected the exact queries that clicked into that single URL on Nov. 2, there were 23 slightly reworded variations of the exact same troubleshooting question (including systematic number permutations like 3 times, 4 times, 5 times, 7 times, 10 times), almost every single one logging 100% CTR (1 click / 1 impression, 2/2, or 3/3) between Position 10.0 and Position 33.0. Other troubleshooting pages on the same site logged 1 click / 1 impression (100% CTR) at Position 45.5 to 54.0.

Normally, when a basic rank tracker or scraper loads a Google search results page, it only generates impressions – not clicks. For Search Console to record a click, a browser or bot has to actually follow (or trigger) Google’s click-tracking link on the search results page.

Looking at the data patterns across these sites, four possibilities stand out:

Because GSC does not let you filter the top performance chart by ranking position or CTR, there is no perfect one-click button to strip only the bot clicks out of your sitewide graph if dozens of pages were affected. However, these three approaches work well depending on how your site was impacted:

If dozens of pages across your whole site spiked on Desktop (like Sites A & B), you cannot exclude every page individually. While filtering the top bar by Device: Mobile obviously hides your legitimate Desktop traffic too, it is a great sanity check to show a client or stakeholder that your Mobile organic trendline is completely normal (on Site A, Mobile clicks were 30 on Nov 2 vs. 27-40 on prior days; on Site B, Mobile clicks were 11 on Nov 2 vs. 11 on Nov 1).

To see the exact pages that were affected on your site:

If you have Claude, Gemini, ChatGPT, or an agentic coding environment (like Antigravity or Cursor) connected to the Google Search Console API or a custom MCP server, you can paste this exact prompt to check all of your sites in seconds:

No. Because this is automated activity rather than human visitors, you do not need to change your pages, disavow anything, or panic when those clicks drop right back down to baseline. Just annotate early November 2026 in your reporting (right alongside GSC’s other recent reporting changes) so you and your clients know why Search Console suddenly showed a vertical jump in clicks!

Ocarina Of Time Remake Producer Explains Why Mario Easter Eggs Were Cut

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time has removed some of the original game’s Super Mario easter eggs to make sure players are as immersed as possible.

If you’re a Zelda veteran or just an easter egg aficionado, then you no doubt know that Ocarina of Time included a few cheeky references to Nintendo’s other juggernaut series, Super Mario. Talon’s design and the Bowser pendant that he and his daughter wear are the obvious ones, but the real treat is a bunch of Mario portraits that can be seen through the windows of Zelda’s courtyard.

Even though the easter egg is really just a few images from Super Mario 64 and Mario Kart 64, it’s still a beloved secret from the original game. Sadly, those portraits are nowhere to be found in the Ocarina of Time remake, which the series’ producer has explained was done to keep players focused on being immersed in Hyrule.

As pointed out by GameXplain founder Andre Segers on Twitter, looking through the windows of Zelda’s courtyard in the Ocarina of Time remake will no longer treat you to some pixely Mario artwork. IGN points out that the windows now give you a sneak peek at the full Threads of Time tapestry, which, while gorgeous, is a bit of a downgrade.

We finally got a chance to play The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake and it's still one of the best games ever made.

GoNintendo actually had the chance to ask Zelda series producer Eiji Aonuma about the Mario portraits being removed from the remake. Aonuma said that some of the game’s developers asked about adding nods and references to the overall timeline, which he shot down so they could focus on making “the best version of Ocarina of Time that we can”.

The producer went on to say that including the portraits in the original game wasn’t “too big a deal”, but that it could “interrupt” the player’s immersion and sense of worldbuilding in the more detailed remake. As Aonuma puts it, if players walk into the courtyard and think “Why is Yoshi there?”, then it could take away from the moment.

It’s not just the Mario reference that’s been cut, as the Skyward Sword easter egg that was added to the 3DS remake is also nowhere to be seen.

Considering how much effort has gone into updating Ocarina of Time and making it more detailed and immersive for a new generation, it’s a fair point. I don’t think the Mario portraits would have really impacted anyone’s experience all that much, but at least we know why they’re missing in action.

Circle reports $865M Q3 revenue – Can USDC challenge Tether’s dominance?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Circle Internet Group (CRCL) reported strong financial performance in Q3 2026, with growth in USDC adoption, transaction activity, revenue, and profitability compared to the same period last year.

As per the financial report, the average amount of USDC in circulation reached $73.3 billion, increasing 8% year-over-year. Meanwhile, USDC’s on-chain transaction volume surged 49% to $13.9 trillion.

At the same time, the company also recorded $865 million in total revenue and reserve income, marking a 17% annual increase. This includes income earned from investing the reserves backing USDC, primarily in cash and short-term U.S. government securities.

On the financial front, Circle generated $658 million in reserve income, down 8% from the previous year. This decline was primarily attributed to a lower reserve return rate of 3.5%, compared to the previous year’s rate, which was 61 basis points higher.

In simple terms, lower returns on the assets backing USDC affected the company’s primary revenue source. However, other revenue increased significantly to $207 million, largely due to the ARC token presale, which contributed $182 million.

All of this happened as Circe went through major developments in Q3 2026. Starting with the launch of Arc’s public mainnet on the 16th of September 2026, which is Circle’s Layer-1 blockchain designed to support financial applications, payments, and institutional transactions. Then Circle also expanded its global payments ecosystem through several partnerships.

The company signed a definitive agreement to acquire Singapore-based cross-border payments platform Tazapay, a deal expected to close in 2027, subject to regulatory approvals. Binance also announced a $100 million equity investment in Circle and renewed its strategic partnership to promote USDC adoption.

Meanwhile, Visa moved its USDC-based stablecoin prefunding solution from pilot testing to commercial deployment, while Samsung Wallet introduced USDC-based international payment capabilities for eligible US users. All of this coincided with Circles’ stock trading at $76.12 after a hike of 1.17% in the past trading session.

Yet, despite all of this, Tether’s USDT still continues to account for the largest share of overall stablecoin transaction activity. As per data from Visa on-chain analytics, USDT recorded $763.77 billion from July to September 2026 as compared to USDC, which recorded $123.73 billion during the same period.

In fact, AMBCrypto also reported that Q4 has started on a weak note, with the stablecoin market cap struggling to cross $310 billion, suggesting limited liquidity across the crypto market.

At the same time, the European Banking Authority (EBA) has designated Circle’s USDC as the first major e-money token under the EU’s MiCA framework. Starting 12th November, 2026, Circle will face stricter supervisory requirements, with the EBA monitoring its capital, reserves, liquidity, and redemption guarantees.

Piece Together the Perfect Pandora Holiday Gift for Her

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

From a little sparkle to a personal engraving, these pieces make a point of showing you care.

Great gifts prove you’ve been paying attention. They show you know what brings her joy, what she reaches for every day, and what she’d never think to buy herself. For the women who matter most in your life, that kind of thoughtfulness goes a long way. Mom, for example, deserves something that thanks her for a lifetime of being your first call when something goes wrong. Your sister craves a piece that feels unmistakably her. Your significant other needs something that says more than a dozen roses ever could. The trick is finding a gift with a little staying power, something sentimental, meaningful, and made to last. Jewelry does all three.

This is where Pandora comes in. Founded in Copenhagen in 1982, the jewelry brand has grown from a small Danish shop into a global name, known for its hand-finished designs and signature charm bracelets.

To make the search easier, we’ve picked five pieces for five important women: your mom, sister, significant other, best friend, and daughter. For each one, these sparkling options will become part of her daily rotation, a marker of a particular milestone, and a reminder that the person who gave it to her got it right. Consider this your holiday cheat sheet.

Your sister has always had a way of stealing the spotlight. Might as well give her the earrings to match. The set pairs sterling silver hoops with baguette-cut stones and detachable star charms, so she can switch up her look depending on the occasion. This means she gets two ways to wear them, because even after a lifetime of knowing her, you’re always discovering another side to her that’s worth celebrating.

Some things are better together, including the two of you. This pendant necklace brings three connected stars to life in sterling silver and 14k gold plating, giving the mixed-metal design a little extra dimension. It’s a thoughtful way to celebrate the person who makes your world brighter, shares your best adventures, and puts up with your quirks. The last part alone deserves a gift.

She may be growing up, but she’ll always be your little princess. This ring gives that title a stylish upgrade, with a golden chevron silhouette and a tapered baguette-cut stone at its center. Crafted in 14k gold plating, it’s a sweet reminder that she shouldn’t have to wait for a royal wedding to wear a tiara.

A good best-friend gift proves you know her taste almost as well as she does. The Sparkling Baguette Tennis Bracelet makes a strong case for that, with a sleek row of baguette-cut stones set in 14k gold plating. It’s polished enough to dress up a special occasion outfit, easy enough for every day, and just the sort of thing she’d pick out herself. Knowing her that well? That’s the real flex.

This tag pendant in sterling silver can be engraved with a date, a few meaningful words, or initials that form a message that needs no explanation to anyone else. She can wear it on its own or layer it with her favorite necklaces. The best part? You get to decide what it says. Mom has spent years remembering the little things about you. Now it’s your turn to give her something personal.

India targets creating homegrown GPUs within 2 years and plans to commercialize indigenous SHAKTI and VEGA processors

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

India targets a homegrown GPU within 24 months as it expands its chip industry and AI ambitions

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

India wants to develop its own graphics processing unit (GPU) within the next two years as the country steps up efforts to build a domestic semiconductor industry and reduce its dependence on foreign chip suppliers, reports ETGovernment. The target was outlined by S.Krishnan, secretary of India's Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology (MeitY), in a November 4 interview. "Under India Semiconductor Mission 2.0, we intend to target the development of an indigenous GPU chip. We should have a GPU chip of our own. This will not happen overnight. It is a project that will require approximately 24 months, and we intend to work towards that objective," Krishnan said.

The proposed GPU is part of a wider plan to establish an Indian hardware and software ecosystem, with Krishnan describing five layers needed to support AI development. These include: energy, accelerator chips, computing infrastructure and servers, AI models and applications. Indian companies such as VVDN, Kaynes and Avalon are already manufacturing servers, while the Centre for Development of Advanced Computing (C-DAC) has developed its Rudra server range, whose systems are designed to support AI workloads, after which the government transfers the technology to local manufacturers. Krishnan also sees AI as a potential repeat of the opportunity India's IT industry experienced during the Y2K (the year 2000 bug) transition. "AI could create a similar opportunity, although the nature of the work will be different," he said.

The comparison is big for a country whose IT-enabled service sector employs about 6 million people. A NITI Aayog paper estimates that roughly two million existing jobs could become redundant as AI adoption changes the industry, but around 4 million more jobs could emerge. Krishnan argues that Indian companies could benefit from helping businesses worldwide integrate into their AI operations, just as they benefited from the demand for software services around the turn of the century.

The country has already attracted major interest with ASML, partnering with Tata Electronics to equip India's first commercial semiconductor fabrication facility in Dholera, Gujarat. The planned facility is expected to use 300mm wafers and eventually reach a capacity of 50,000 wafers per month. Intel also announced a strategic partnership with Tata Group covering semiconductor manufacturing and packaging for the Indian market. However, the proposed GPU remains an early-stage target. Krishnan did not identify a manufacturer, chip supplier, or builder for the project. India is also looking to bring its own homegrown SHAKTI and VEGA processors to commercial markets. Both are based on the open-source RISC-V architecture, allowing local developers to build processors without relying on proprietary CPU designs. Notably, these are CPU designs, separate from the GPUs now being targeted by MeitY.

That said, it is not the first time India has attempted to build its own GPU production unit. In 2019, India's IT Madras released a software development kit that allowed developers to begin building applications before the processors were commercialized. The project included several processor classes aimed at different markets, from embedded devices and micro-controllers to workstations and server workloads. The effort has continued since then, with the Aries 3.0 development board, which uses the C-DAC’s VEGA ET301 processor, being introduced in 2024. The 32-bit chip runs up to 100MHz and includes 26KB of SRAM and is intended for relatively simple applications such as sensors, wearables, and Internet of Things devices, rather than desktop PCs or graphics-intensive workloads.

However, there are signs that the SHAKTI and VEGA platforms are nodding beyond development boards and into practical applications. At the September 26 SEMICOM India event, the government highlighted projects using the processors, including an autonomous water-cleaning robot around SHAKTI and a digital telescope using VEGA. Another VEGA-based project was designed to monitor forest environments. The government's Design -linked Incentive (DLI) program is already supporting more of that work. MeiyT says 24 companies received assistance under the scheme, and subsequently raised venture capital or other private investment. Their projects include microcontrollers, drone flight controllers, smart meter chips and CCTV cameras.

India's semiconductor market is currently valued at just about $50billion and is expected to approach 100 billion by 2030, according to a report by the India Electronics and Semiconductor Association (IESA). The government wants local companies to capture more of that growth domestically rather than remain dependent on imported components.

Studio Shea designs South Carolina library as "community living room"

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Photography by Keith Isaacs (unless stated otherwise)

Brooklyn-based practice Studio Shea has completed a metal-clad library with a central atrium that draws on the civic and industrial context of its site in South Carolina, USA.

Clinton Public Library serves a railroad junction town of less than 8,000 residents.

The 11,000-square-foot (1,021-square metre) library translates the vernacular porch feature as well as the town’s agricultural and textile manufacturing heritage into a contemporary public building, Justin Shea, founder of Studio Shea, told Dezeen.

“At its core, the project was conceived as a welcoming community living room,” Shea said.

Set on 1.25 acres, the building’s scale and massing respond to its surrounding residential context in view of the town’s downtown corridor.

“The deep roof overhang creates a shaded front porch that recalls the familiar social role of the porch as a place for gathering,” Shea said, explaining that the low-pitched roofs and striated standing-seam metal cladding are a subtle nod to the area’s material character.

“Together, these elements give the library a contemporary identity while maintaining a clear connection to the character and history of Clinton.”

Inside, the library centres around a daylit atrium that is both the social and organisational heart of the program spaces and collections.

From the entrance, library patrons can see through the atrium to the collections, study areas, activity spaces and reading garden in a clear, easy-to-navigate scheme.

“Particular attention was given to the quality of natural light,” said Shea.

“A continuous clerestory draws soft, even daylight deep into the building, activating the interior throughout the day. At night, the illuminated atrium reverses that relationship, allowing the library to glow outward as a beacon for the community.”

The plan was designed for adaptability and flexibility, with the makerspace, children’s room, and meeting areas expanding into the center for larger workshops and the community room connecting with the lobby, service spaces and exterior plaza for after-hours events.

Inside, pops of colour, monochromatic spaces and varied textures create a playful environment suited to a broad range of ages and activities, while generous glazing contributes to the sense of openness.

A key element of the design is a community bench along the building’s exterior – designed by Studio Shea and fabricated with oak trees from the site by Piedmont Technical College – that ties into the lobby bench, circulation desk and collection shelving.

“What might otherwise have been an incidental site resource became a tangible expression of local participation and investment,” Shea explained.

“Public architecture can create a stronger sense of ownership when the community is given opportunities to participate in its creation,” he continued.

“A civic building does not need to be monumental to be significant; it can achieve meaning through being welcoming, useful, adaptable and genuinely connected to the people and place it serves.”

Other public buildings recently completed in South Carolina include a county administration building with “crisp edges” by Foster + Partners, the International African American Museum that floats above water by Pei Cobb Freed & Partners and Moody Nolan and a seaside restaurant designed for nostalgia by SDCO and Basic Projects.

How TF is the grocery store a media channel?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This WTF guide, sponsored by Grocery TV, explores how marketers are reimagining in-store retail media to engage consumers inside the grocery store.

Even with the growth of digital channels such as e-commerce, social and CTV, the physical store remains one of the most influential moments in the path to purchase.

According to Grocery TV’s 2026 In-Store Shopper Perception Report, 95% of grocery shoppers make at least half of their purchase decisions in-store. Even younger, in-demand audiences frequent physical retail locations, with 55% of Gen Z consumers shopping in-store at least once a week.

Despite this, marketers have historically treated the store more like a retail backdrop than a media channel. Media buyers, especially digital media buyers, have long defaulted to other channels that were easier to tap into. For digital buyers accustomed to unified inventory, real-time optimization and closed-loop attribution, in-store was the opposite: a fragmented, retailer-by-retailer negotiation with no standardized metrics.

Now, in-store can be bought through a single platform, targeted with the precision of digital and measured across the funnel, making it possible to plan against the store with more sophistication.

Investment is following, with eMarketer projecting that U.S. in-store retail media spending will cross the billion-dollar milestone to reach $1.15 billion by 2029.

“We’re seeing advertisers lean in, testing and learning what works now that the infrastructure and measurement capabilities are in place,” said Marlow Nickell, co-founder and CEO of Grocery TV. “There’s a lot of opportunity ahead as more retailers launch their in-store networks.”

In this WTF explainer guide, Digiday and Grocery TV examine how savvy brands, retailers and agencies are leveraging the grocery store as a media channel. The majority of brand choice is made in a retail environment — not on TV or online — and now is the time for nonendemic and CPG marketers alike to rethink how they use the grocery store to engage audiences.

In-store grocery media uses digital screens placed throughout grocery stores to reach shoppers during their shopping trips.

The channel combines the strengths of three other established media channels:  the reach and context of DOOH, the storytelling capabilities of video and the audience and commerce connection of retail media. These qualities come together inside the physical grocery environment, reaching consumers during their routine grocery shopping trip.

Nickell sees proximity to the purchase decision as the key distinction. “With digital out-of-home, it’s often more on-the-go media, like a billboard you’re driving past. In-store retail media is much closer to the point of purchase, where shoppers are making decisions and considering different products,” said Nickell.

Chelsea Monaco, svp, commerce media, Digitas North America, noted that this blending of channels makes classification tricky. “In-store retail media today can be categorized multiple ways, and it varies by the organization,” she said.

“The intersection of digital and in-store blurs in the retail media space, and the investment sources are so unique,” Monaco said. “I’ve typically seen it most categorized as shopper marketing, but investment source and where it sits in the organization depict the true categorization.”

Grocery trips offer more opportunities for product discovery than checking items off a list might suggest. Shoppers can encounter new products, respond to promotions and make unplanned decisions as they browse the aisles.

According to Grocery TV’s In-Store Shopper Perception 2026 report, 32% of shoppers enter the store without a list, and 78% make at least one impulse purchase during their trip.

In-store media can influence those decisions. Grocery TV research found that 62% of shoppers have purchased something directly after seeing it on an in-store screen. Screens are also shoppers’ top way to learn about sales, promotions and new products, allowing brands to directly engage shoppers at the point of purchase.

“When you ask people what they like most about grocery shopping, usually the answer is the discovery. They like the fact that they can see and hold things, and really learn about new products,” Nickell said. “That’s why we find shoppers are open to advertising. They’re already in a buying mindset.”

That opportunity is only growing. In 2026, shopper receptivity increased across every major store zone compared to Grocery TV’s 2023 study. Digital display formats saw an average 10-percentage-point increase in positive customer experience ratings, climbing from 41% to 51%. This suggests that in-store media can be relevant to a broad range of grocery shoppers.

Grocery retailers with in-store media networks run digital screens throughout the store, concentrated in high-traffic and high-dwell spots like entrances and checkout where shoppers pass through or wait. Brands match their creative to the products and audiences they want to reach, such as a financial services brand connecting with shoppers as they wait at checkout.

“The screen is physically there in the environment,” Nickell said. “You don’t have to worry about an ad being blocked or scrolled out of view in the same way you do online.”

Where screens appear also matters. Research from Grocery TV shows that shoppers are more receptive to screens in high-dwell areas, where they naturally spend time waiting, such as the pharmacy, deli and checkout. Shoppers rated these zones as 84% favorable, according to Grocery TV’s 2026 In-Store Shopper Perception Report.

Entrances are another opportunity, as shoppers are accustomed to seeing signage there. By contrast, placements that interrupt the shopping experience, such as cooler screens, tend to be less popular.

Effective in-store creative reflects both the product being advertised and the context in which shoppers encounter it. Brands can tailor messaging to the shopping environment, seasonal needs or specific audiences.

Nickell encourages brands to advertise across the entire store, with creative that connects to the shopping environment and gives shoppers a reason to engage beyond their planned purchases.

For instance, a snack brand might build a campaign around a game-day occasion, using creative that inspires shoppers to add something to their basket whether they see it entering the store or waiting at checkout. Education-forward content, such as recipes or tips for choosing an insurance carrier, can also give shoppers something useful to engage with during their visit.

Shoppers are 2.5 times more likely to consider a brand when an ad is contextually relevant to its environment, according to research from Grocery TV. The surrounding context can also shape how shoppers perceive an ad: 84% viewed end-cap ads featuring contextually relevant creative favorably or neutrally, compared with 79% for ads that were unrelated to the environment.

“As an industry, we often focus on the capability or the creative, but we forget about the overall experience,” Monaco said. “The biggest miss that I’ve seen is not connecting the entire shopper journey or that experience in how that shopper is going to engage with your brand in-store. What experience do you want that shopper to have with your brand at that touchpoint, and how does that fit in with all of the other omnichannel touchpoints they might experience?”

Brands getting started can adapt existing creative from channels such as CTV or social, making adjustments for the in-store environment. Text should be easy to read from a distance, and branding should be prominent. Over time, brands can develop creative specifically for in-store placements, incorporating context such as location, retailer and time of day.

As in-store media has evolved, audience targeting has become more sophisticated, bringing it closer to the strategies marketers already use in TV and digital campaigns. By combining geographic, demographic and behavioral signals with the context of the physical store, advertisers can reach shoppers close to the point of purchase with greater precision.

Geographic targeting can narrow a campaign by state, designated market area (DMA), ZIP code or point of interest. A brand looking to reach students and other campus communities, for example, could target grocery stores near universities.

Behavioral signals, such as purchase patterns and responsiveness to promotions, can help brands refine their audiences further. Demographic signals like age, income and household composition offer another layer. Brands can also prioritize stores that over-index for specific shopper groups, such as Hispanic consumers or older adults.

For example, a telecom brand promoted its international access plan across Grocery TV’s retailer-owned network in stores that over-index for Hispanic shoppers. The campaign drove a 27% lift in consideration for the brand when shopping for a wireless plan, a 15% lift in purchase intent and a 12% lift in unaided brand awareness.

As in-store media becomes easier to plan and buy, marketers have more ways to evaluate its impact, from brand awareness and consideration to incremental sales and repeat purchase. Yet measurement hasn’t always kept pace with the range of outcomes the channel can influence. A 2026 Grocery TV and IAB study found that, across four of five key outcomes, more marketers believe in-store media can drive those outcomes than they can currently measure those outcomes.

Part of the challenge is operational. Brands can struggle to align internal KPIs and standardize measurement across campaigns, while ownership of in-store media still sits largely with shopper marketing teams. The marketers getting the most from in-store take a broader approach, measuring beyond immediate sales and involving national media and brand teams.

Measurement should start with the campaign objective, using tailored methodologies to track different outcomes:

Together, these approaches help advertisers assess performance across the funnel, using methods suited to the outcome they want to understand.

For example, a Grocery TV campaign for a financial health app measured brand impact against a control group, finding an 82% lift in brand rating, a 72% lift in recommendation and a 34% lift in brand awareness. The results show how in-store campaigns can be evaluated against objectives beyond immediate product sales.

“For banking, it makes a lot of sense to run ads at checkout. That’s when you’re pulling cards out of your wallet and making what, for most people, is the largest purchase of the week. Personal finances are already top of mind,” Nickell said.

Sales can be measured, too. In a campaign for Frito-Lay, Hy-Vee’s RedMedia and Grocery TV used a matched-store study across 103 stores, comparing test and control groups over a month. The campaign generated a 15% sales lift and a $7.94 return on ad spend.

Transaction-based measurement can also reveal effects beyond the advertised product. One national personal care brand working with Grocery TV saw a 15% sales lift for the advertised product, a 12% lift across complementary products and an 8% lift across its full brand portfolio.

The opportunity is to measure outcomes aligned with the campaign’s goals, including what happens after the initial purchase. In the IAB study, 75% of marketers who said they fully leverage in-store media measured post-purchase outcomes such as loyalty and repeat purchase, compared with 42% of those who said they underutilize the channel. Measuring beyond immediate sales can give marketers a fuller picture of the role in-store media plays in driving customer retention.

The grocery store can play a complementary role in an integrated plan, connecting brand messaging across channels with the moment shoppers are making purchase decisions. It starts with a clear business objective: What does the campaign need to achieve, and where could in-store contribute?

That may mean reconsidering how budgets are allocated across channels. “The ultimate question is what business outcome will drive the most success,” Monaco said. “We’ve seen cases where it might make sense to take budget out of underperforming digital and move it to in-store.”

A study from Grocery TV and MFour found that in-store media delivers an average of 49% incremental reach beyond existing media plans. Complementing campaigns on other channels with in-store media allows marketers to reduce overexposure by diversifying audiences. They can also maximize media dollars by driving new engagement and closing gaps left by other channels, such as display and online video.

Marketers don’t need to rethink their entire media mix to explore the channel, however. They can start with a defined objective, test in-store alongside other media and evaluate its incremental contribution to outcomes like brand lift or sales. Those findings can help determine where future investment will have the greatest impact.

In-store retail media is still developing as a full-funnel media-buying channel, giving early adopters an edge before it becomes a standard part of media planning. As Nickell noted, brands that start testing now can learn which creative works, how to measure success and how to make the case for investment internally. Those learnings take time to build. “The earlier you get started, the better,” Nickell said.

In the wake of LiveRamp’s acquisition by Publicis, some marketers are auditioning alternatives to the identity graph company.

OpenAI has ambitious ad revenue targets, which depend on advertiser demand, measurement and monetisation.

Agencies and marketers now frequently use AI tools to create media and creative briefs. But the practice could be a canary in the coalmine for AI overuse in advertising.

The Legend of Khiimori's Mongolian Expanse update brings in frosty mountains and scorching deserts that'll require a lot more than a fur coat and a sturdy horse

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Another day, another opportunity for some horsing around! I'm being literal here, to be clear, as a new update for The Legend of Khiimori dropped yesterday. It's called The Mongolian Expanse, opening up the map a whole lot more and making a sweep of other additions to the game.

Previously, you might have looked out unto the snowy mountainscapes and the scorching deserts and thought "boy howdy am I glad I can't go there, those temperatures look like a nightmare to deal with!" Well, good news, as you now can head to those locales, each with "their own waystations, Yam nodes, named places, chests and bandit caches."

You don't want to wander there without some amount of prep, however, as the mountains are cold and difficult to climb, with wolves none too kind to humans like you. The desert, of course, is really hot, for both you and your horse. Both additions remind me of Zelda: Breath of the Wild and its use of extreme temperatures acting as a sort of challenge gate. Neat stuff!

The buck doesn't stop there with this update though. You'll find a whole host of new wildlife, ranging from wild boars, to gobi bears and camels. Of course, a horse game wouldn't be one without more horse, which there is! It's a new breed called the Marwari, a horse that is "built for endurance and especially resistant to heat, a strong partner for long, punishing Desert crossings." Beyond that, you'll also find more than 60 new horse skills, all findable across specific regions and for specific breeds.

It's a nice beefy update with plenty more tweaks, additions and fixes on top of everything mentioned above. When it comes to the mountains and desert, you can also expect some big additions there, as this is only the first part of this update. You can read about everything else the update offers right here on Steam.

Crypto Market Trims $140 Billion as BTC Slips Below $76K

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

In just four hours, another massive crypto crash wiped out $140 billion in market cap as BTC crumbled below the key $76K support level on Nov. 5.

The significant market retracement wiped out a huge part of recent gains, sending a massive wave of liquidations across major crypto exchanges.

At its lowest point, Bitcoin fell to around $75,900 as selling pressure ramped up, unwinding its recent rebound above the $80K mark on Nov. 4.

The recent crypto market crash also coincides with a sudden drop in U.S. tech stocks, further concerns over Fed monetary policy, and uncertainty over the implications of the U.S. midterm elections for regulation.

As per data from cryptocurrency derivatives analytics service CoinGlass, a huge sum of $1.05B in leveraged crypto positions has been liquidated in the last 24 hours, impacting 203,418 crypto traders.

The vast majority of liquidations involved long positions, with about $926.81 million (88%) wiped out, while shorts saw roughly $126.60 million (12%) liquidated.

In the last four hours, a total of $872.48 million in positions were wiped out as selling pressure peaked.

This includes $793M in longs and only $79.48 million in shorts.

In the last hour, there were $228.90 million in liquidations, with $196.52 million in longs.

The biggest single liquidation recorded by CoinGlass was on Binance, where a long ETH/USDT position was liquidated for roughly $10.94 million at $2,243.86.

As reported by Reuters, the Nasdaq Composite index slid toward correction levels on Thursday as investors continued to review the profitability of large investments in artificial intelligence (AI).

The stocks of major semiconductor and AI-related firms, such as Nvidia, AMD, Qualcomm, and others, were hammered in the process.

The crypto market is becoming more sensitive to tech stock movements. As per the recent Bitfinex Alpha report, the 30-day correlation between Bitcoin and the Nasdaq 100 climbed to 0.68.

This shows a strong correlation between these two markets.

In the meantime, the crypto space is still dealing with the aftermath of the huge security breach at Binance at the end of October, with around $6.3B in stolen assets.

If BTC fails to hold this support area, selling could accelerate, especially if U.S. equities remain in their current state.

The Generosity of Selfishness: C.S. Lewis on How to Be Interesting

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

You can also become a spontaneous supporter with a one-time donation in any amount:

Partial to Bitcoin? You can beam some bit-love my way: 197usDS6AsL9wDKxtGM6xaWjmR5ejgqem7

Need to cancel a recurring donation? Go here.

For an act of resistance to the tyranny of algorithms, try the Marginalian newsletter—undistracted notes on the search for meaning, free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human since 2006. (Here is an example.)

Our lives are defined not by what we have but what we give — on what and whom we spend our energy and time, how we share our material possessions and our mental powers, what we do with our gifts. “Anything you do not give freely and abundantly becomes lost to you,” Annie Dillard wrote in contemplating the nature of creativity. “You open your safe and find ashes.”

Paradoxically, the ability to give hinges on the ability to receive: You must be rested in order to labor, taking the repose it takes to recharge your vital energies; you must be interested in order to be interesting, taking the world in with a greedy curiosity that absorbs its beauty and strangeness in order to metabolize it into meaning.

C.S. Lewis (November 29, 1898–November 22, 1963) offers a wonderfully counterintuitive perspective on our notions of generosity and our ideas about what it means to be interesting in his memoir Surprised by Joy (public library).

With an eye to the ideal structure of the creative life, he writes:

It is a life almost entirely selfish. Selfish, not self-centred: for in such a life my mind would be directed towards a thousand things, not one of which is myself. The distinction is not unimportant. One of the happiest men and most pleasing companions I have ever known was intensely selfish. On the other hand I have known people capable of real sacrifice whose lives were nevertheless a misery to themselves and to others, because self-concern and self-pity filled all their thoughts. Either condition will destroy the soul in the end. But till the end, give me the man who takes the best of everything (even at my expense) and then talks of other things, rather than the man who serves me and talks of himself, and whose very kindnesses are a continual reproach, a continual demand for pity, gratitude, and admiration.

This is also why, Lewis intimates, the “thrill” of our greatest creative breakthroughs — those revelations that give us back to ourselves, out of which we give our best work to the world — is rooted in “self-forgetfulness”:

Only when your whole attention and desire are fixed on something else — whether a distant mountain, or the past, or the gods of Asgard — does the “thrill” arise.

Shut your mouth; open your eyes and ears. Take in what is there.

Couple with John Steinbeck on the art of receiving, then revisit C.S. Lewis on what we long for in our existential longing.

I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. Lionel Messi left international football with grace. Cristiano Ronaldo didn't...

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Messi retired from international football at the right time, writes Will Castle, while his supposed ‘GOAT debate’ counterpart continued to chip away at his own legacy before eventually throwing in the towel

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

In boxing, some say the classiest way to go out is on your back, staring at the lights. Everyone loves a fairytale ending, but there is a certain closure that comes with bowing out in defeat.

Despite scoring in his final international outing in Buenos Aires, a 3-0 friendly win over Benin, Lionel Messi ended his Argentina career in a similar vein. The final, overriding taste was one of loss.

The eight-time Ballon d’Or winner, 39, announced his retirement from international football following the 2026 World Cup. His designated farewell match was held at the the iconic Estadio Monumental, home of River Plate.

Widely regarded as the greatest footballer of all time, Messi goes out having seen his stock rise even further in the summer. He completely outperformed expectation, even for him, at the World Cup to show that he could still run the show while pushing 40. Eight goals, four assists and, briefly, the tournament’s all-time top scorer - before golden boot winner Kylian Mbappe snatched that accolade - represented a truly remarkable output for the Argentinian magician, even if it all came to nothing.

After guiding the defending champions to a second successive World Cup final, he was unable to repeat his heroics of Qatar to end on the highest of highs. In fact, it was the lowest of lows. Argentina’s post-match brawl sparked by Leandro Paredes and co. cast a cloud of shame over him and an entire nation, though Messi was not an innocent party in Argentina’s performance of disgrace.

In the aftermath of Enzo Fernandez ragdolling Pau Cubarsi to get himself sent off in second-half stoppage time, he tried to level the playing field after Marc Cucurella spoke while covering his mouth, appealing to the referee to produce a red under the “Vinicius law” - something that had been written into the game courtesy of compatriot Gianluca Prestianni’s alleged racism. It was a desperate abandonment of sporting integrity from a footballing marvel made out to be immune to mistakes.

His efforts both in this regard and with the football at his feet proved fruitless. Spain ran out deserving winners against Argentina’s anti-football in extra time.

Messi’s tears on New Jersey shores spoke to an inevitability. Though with less individual ignominy than Diego Maradona before him, whose international retirement followed a failed drug test at the 1994 World Cup, Messi was similarly hurtling towards the end of his tenure as Argentina’s heroic figurehead. That was rubber stamped by the passing of his father and agent, Jorge, at the age of 68 after a long illness.

Six weeks after the final, Messi confirmed his retirement. “There isn’t much time left, chapters come to a close, and this is one that hurts me deeply,” he wrote on Instagram. “I love, have loved, and will always love being part of the national team. I've given it my all, I have nothing left to give, and besides, there are some really great young players coming through behind me who deserve their chance."

It felt right. After 21 years, 207 appearances, 125 goals and 68 assists, there will be just one more game. An international career categorised by pain and perseverance, he bows out with a World Cup, two Copa Americas and an Olympic gold medal to his name; though in contrast to his club success, he more often fell at the final hurdle. Runners-up medals at two World Cups and three Copa Americas, heartbreak regularly prevailed during the Messi era - something that eventually became the rocket fuel for the current crop of players, dedicated to deliver him those titles before it was too late. Lionel Scaloni built a team that would first and foremost die for Messi; that team won Argentina’s first World Cup since Mexico ‘86 and third overall.

He retired having achieved what he wanted, and he retired an undisputed hero. The same cannot be said for his “GOAT debate” counterpart, if you can even call him that anymore, with Cristiano Ronaldo’s coming to the most pathetic of ends thanks to his latest strop.

After at least two years of being a hinderance to the team’s efforts, taking up a starting spot that should be going to someone younger and more deserving, Ronaldo opted against using the natural end of the World Cup cycle to hang up his international boots and instead remained in the fold. That was until he quit the squad due to alleged broken promises by manager Jorge Jesus, which revolved around being left as an unused substitute for the first time in 18 years for a Nations League clash with Norway; one Portugal won, with his replacement Goncalo Ramos scoring the winner.

The thing is, nobody actually cares anymore. Ronaldo is like that self-important wet flannel you kind of know that posts: “Deleting my social media, had enough of fake people. If you need me, text me.” Except he’s not 14, he’s 41.

It seems like he actually received that message of football’s collective eye-roll, because after being fined the paltry sum of £215 and suspended for 30 days by the Portuguese FA, Ronaldo has now decided to call time on his 23-year Portugal career. He leaves as international football’s all-time top goalscorer and top appearance maker, yet his retirement comes with an avoidable sour taste in the mouth; like someone took a sledgehammer to a sculpture. One of the undisputed icons of this sport has undoubtedly eroded his legacy because of the late-career antics, though it is a relief to see him finally come to his senses on the Portugal front in an act of damage control.

Then there’s his rival, who has watched silently as Ronaldo kept hammering nail after nail into the coffin that is his “GOAT” argument. Messi’s continues to glisten, despite being fresh off failure on the grandest of stages, both on an individual and team level. He has more than enough credit in the bank for that to be forgiven.

Many in Portugal would probably say the same for Ronaldo. After all, he captained the country to a first major title at Euro 2016, and is far and away their all-time top scorer with 146 goals in 234 games. But the pitfalls of his personality have become increasingly hard to defend, even if “hard to defend” isn’t exactly a phrase you’d associate Ronaldo with at the top level anymore.

Both footballers have defined an era of football to a point of deification in their respective homelands. It’s just that Messi left at the right time, with dignity and his head held high; while Ronaldo threw in the towel after clinging on for too long.

The on-chain data and the price chart are pointing in different directions. Whales and exchange outflows suggest quiet accumulation, while the price is still being pulled down by the wider market rout. For now, holding $1.21 to $1.24 is critical. A move above $1.34 would be the first real sign that buyers are back in control.

Airbnb Plans AI Travel Agent for Planning, Booking and Support

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Airbnb plans to begin rolling out an AI agent for trip planning and booking to guests in the first half of 2027, the company said in its third-quarter 2026 shareholder letter released November 5, 2026.

The product remains in development. Airbnb’s longer-term goal is for the specialized agent to plan an entire trip across homes, hotels, services, experiences and additional categories, with discovery, booking and post-booking assistance handled within Airbnb.

Rather than relying only on a conventional text chat window, the planned experience builds an interface around a guest’s question. The agent draws from a library of hundreds of travel-specific interface elements, including maps, photos, comparisons, calendars and itineraries, and composes them in real time according to what the guest wants.

That design is intended to let guests explore options, make decisions with travel companions and take action directly in Airbnb. It reflects the company’s position that discovering where to go and where to stay is part of the travel experience, unlike routine tasks such as making a restaurant reservation or scheduling an appointment.

Airbnb reports that more than 75% of guests use its maps when deciding where to stay, over 90% browse multiple listing photos and 70% read reviews before booking. The average booking has three guests, making the choice of a place to stay a shared decision for many trips and helping explain why the proposed agent emphasizes browsing and comparisons instead of only answering questions.

Airbnb cites nearly 20 years of bringing millions of hosts and guests together as the foundation for a personalized travel search and booking experience that outside agents cannot replicate. More than 90% of stays involve direct guest-host communication, with verified identities and reviews helping build trust.

Reservation control is another advantage. Because Airbnb handles reservations, payments and customer support, the planned agent is intended to change reservations, resolve payment issues, assist with refunds and rebooking, and provide access to 24-hour safety support. Airbnb argues that an outside agent cannot easily perform those integrated actions on its own.

Several guest-facing pieces are already available: natural-language or voice search across homes, experiences and services; AI-suggested filters; AI-generated listing summaries; and side-by-side wishlist comparisons with AI summaries. They show Airbnb applying AI to individual parts of discovery, while the planned agent extends that work into a generated interface and post-booking actions.

Airbnb’s separate customer-support assistant is available in more than 50 languages and is expanding to voice support in the United States. The company reports that approximately 40% of all Q3 2026 customer-support issues were resolved without a human agent, up from one-third in Q2 2026. That figure covers every support issue, including those that did not start with the assistant.

Airbnb attributes a year-over-year decline of more than 17% in Q3 customer-support cost per booking largely to assistant improvements. The result gives the agent plan an operational backdrop: automation is already resolving service requests and reducing support expense, while the new product would bring AI into the booking and trip-management workflow.

Airbnb also reports shipping more than 50 products and features in Q3, nearly 70% more than a year earlier, with AI helping teams move faster. Quarterly revenue was $4.9 billion, up 19% year over year. Nights and Seats Booked, which combines nights booked for stays with seats booked for services and experiences after cancellations and alterations, reached 155.0 million, up 16%.

The plan joins two distinct uses of AI: cutting support handling costs and shaping the guest’s travel workflow. Its central product tension is that Airbnb wants the agent to act throughout a trip while preserving the browsing, comparison and group decision-making that its own guest figures describe as integral to booking.

Russell Brand Has Been Found Guilty. American Courts Could Learn a Lot From His Trial

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Britain’s most notorious serial killers and infamous gangsters are convicted at London’s Old Bailey, under a neo-Baroque dome and gold-gilded statue of Lady Justice. But it is Southwark Crown Court, an entirely bland heap of 1980s bricks by the River Thames, where the embattled entertainers wash up.

Only recently, this is where rapper Chris Brown—who pleaded guilty to affray after striking a man in the head with a glass bottle in a nightclub—learned he would avoid prison. It is also where Russell Brand today was found guilty of one count of rape and three of sexual assault. He was found not guilty of two other counts of rape and one count of attempted indecent assault.

The week before, whilst reporting on Brand’s trial, I watched a fan in the corridor outside the courtroom admiringly accost the comedian. “I’ve been told to tell you there are many who support you,” she told him. He gave a zen reply: “I receive it, I receive it.” But by and large, English courts are not interested in fame.

In England, the court is an uncontaminated information space: There are no cameras to broadcast what happens inside, and there are strict rules to marshal what information can come in. It may be one of the few places on earth where an influencer can no longer influence; even Katy Perry, Brand’s ex-wife, had to be explained to the judge. In 2026, the closest the US has come to creating a similar environment is Taylor Swift’s wedding.

In a world of video clips, this courtroom is unclippable. What happens here is beyond the reach of rubbernecking pundits or home sleuths wanting a hot take for their socials. Unlike in modern discourse, there are rules in this court, and they are strictly enforced: Complainants in sexual cases are entitled to immediate and lifelong anonymity in the UK. Their real names and identifying details were heard in court—but mention them outside, and you risk arrest for contempt of court. Here, the defense also must ask the judge’s permission to bring up the accusers’ sexual history. When it was suspected that one blogger may have crossed the line, the judge barked at the lead prosecutor: “You’ve plenty of police officers back there…. Send one to arrest him!” They promptly did, and he was ultimately released with a caution.

Jurors, too, can never be unmasked, and their deliberations are forever secret. I sat watching them throughout the trial, but can never tell you what I observed.

From the initial charging to the conclusion of the trial, we in the British media are limited to reporting fairly and accurately only what the jury hears each day. Anyone weighing in publicly with an opinion, a prediction, or anything that could create a “substantial risk of serious prejudice or impediment” to the trial risks joining Brand in the dock of a criminal court. This meant there was no spin as Brand stepped down from the witness stand—no online reprisals against his accusers.

In court, Brand described himself as someone who had once been “staggeringly” famous. His defense argued that, in 2023, reporting from Channel 4, The Times, and The Sunday Times alleging sexual misbehavior changed that—and made everyone desert him. This, Brand said, is why only three witnesses were called to support him—two, if you exclude his wife.

“It’s not the case you’ve been completely abandoned,” wily prosecutor Riel Karmy-Jones stated in cross-examination on October 28. Eight seconds of silent staring was Brand’s reply.

“You have a platform with followers in the millions, don’t you?” Silence.

“Yes,” he eventually returned, albeit quietly. Even Harold Pinter would have shouted, “Get on with it.”

Keeping up? To follow any of this is to wade through a thicket of non-sequitur arguments, florid vocabulary and, in his latest twist, the Gospels.

How has Brand’s flimflam, wrapped up in the needy personality of an entertainer who lives off clicks, landed in England’s criminal court system? Uncomfortably, I would say. Southwark is dilapidated and definitely analogue. The litigators wore robes and horsehair wigs, and the microphone on the prosecutor’s lectern was held in place with an enormous amount of yellow and black hazard duct tape. The lights went out twice because of a power cut.

If the world is now video-first, our courts are video-last. With the occasional exception of a judge’s sentencing remarks, nothing in court is photographed, filmed, or recorded for the media. Even the court artist can only take written notes, and must do their actual sketches outside.

“It’s not theater,” I heard the court clerk tell a law student who’d come to watch Brand’s trial but was annoyed to find the six public seats were full.

If this case had been undertaken in Brand’s adopted home of Florida, everyone’s evidence would have been tailored to win over cable news panel pundits. Soundbites would trump reasoned long-form arguments. The names of his accusers would likely be public—as would anything else anyone could dig up about them. Did the framers of the American Constitution really want @truecrimesleuthboy unpicking old Facebook albums of women alleging rape, or network news cameras hounding jurors on their decision?

Did you watch the Amber Heard–versus–Johnny Depp defamation trial in 2022? Were you one of the billions using #AmberTurd online as the “courtroom porn,” as Monica Lewinsky called it for Vanity Fair, was dissected, criticized, and memed? After her defeat, Heard’s lawyer spoke out in frustration: “There’s no way [the jury] couldn’t have been influenced” by the online discussion, she said. “It was horrible.”

Because of where Brand’s trial took place, his testimony could never have been turned into a similar show. In Court 3, there was no camera awaiting his reactions to what he was allegedly being subjected to. Maybe that’s why he didn’t try to pull any of that.

Brand did arrive at court with a Holy Bible bursting with index tabs. One day, when stuck in the corridor with the assembled press pack, he also uncomfortably boomed out, “This is a great day.” But for the most part, I observed him sitting tall and still and lonely in the dock. His personal bodyguard dozed in the courtroom’s family and friends section as an assistant scrolled through her phone and his father demolished a Snickers.

The only sign his group was listening to the awful things their friend/boss/son was being accused of was the very active facial expressions of Brand’s wife, Laura. “Oh, come on,” she appeared to mouth on one occasion, listening to one woman alleging rape.

There was no sign of Brand the comedian or Brand the YouTuber when the defendant took to the witness stand—though he did begin his testimony defiantly, answering the first question with a “No” so loud he seemed to shock even himself. From then on, he diminuendoed so far that the judge jumped in: “You are starting to drop your voice.”

Brand’s silences began when prosecution advocate Karmy-Jones took to her feet. A distinguished prosecutor and defender, she’s worked with and around murderers, sex offenders, and organized crooks. She’s also done time with terrible showoffs, having begun her career as a theater director. Kristin Scott Thomas or a young Lauren Bacall might play her in the film version of the trial.

Karmy-Jones was achingly polite as she unpicked what Brand called his “escalating recollection” of events. In four police interviews across 18 months, claimed Brand, he had no memory of the six women accusing him—even when given photographs, names, and some details. The British version of the Miranda warning tells suspects under caution, “You do not have to say anything. But it may harm your defense if you do not mention when questioned something which you later rely on in court.”

According to the police tapes, so-called Woman 3 did not ring a bell for him back then. But in the witness box, Brand described meeting her at a bar, recalling that a friend had said something like, “There’s a bird here who really wants to meet you.” He remembered that she had “prominent eyes,” was “lithe, elfic, pixyish,” and consented to go to the toilet for “kissing..fondling.” He was found guilty of sexually assaulting her, and acquitted on that charge of rape.

“Are you sure you aren’t piecing an account together from the evidence that was served?” Karmy-Jones asked.

Maybe the lack of spotlight and vodcaster microphone lowered his guard. This proud wordsmith did not seem ready for his words to be used against him. He wouldn’t have sexually assaulted a woman he was flirting with, Brand said. He would have been “complimentary, flirtatious, tactile.” Yet when Brand tried to drift into generalities, he was dragged back to specifics.

“Not if you met someone, Mr Brand. What was the conversation you had?” What did he mean when he said he would have been “tactile?” “Tactile means touch,” was his gently smug reply.

“Indeed it does,” Karmy-Jones returned, dragging him back to the allegation that he had grabbed the woman’s breasts.

When was the last time Brand had been spoken to like this? He did not seem to enjoy it. He even conceded there were “small details” in his evidence “that are inconsistent, and I acknowledge that.”

Perhaps you can see why Brand and his advocates didn’t attempt any “this is a deep state conspiracy sparked by my client’s vlogging about Pfizer” material on the witness stand. During the trial, he somehow managed to maneuver himself into a difficult position: insisting that despite not remembering it previously, he did have a conversation with Woman 5 about consent in which she agreed to unprotected anal sex, even though she’d just insisted their consensual vaginal sex involve a condom. “Nonsense, wasn’t it, Mr. Brand?” asked Karmy-Jones. The jury must have agreed. Brand was found guilty of this charge.

The jury was given the space to examine all this without any public discourse crowding their own thoughts out of their head. And it’s clear to the rest of us that this is preferable: A Gallup poll this year suggested just 17% of Americans have confidence in their criminal justice system. Compare that to the latest Crime Survey for England and Wales, which found 48% have confidence in how criminal justice is administered.

Our courts need physical repair, better pay, more money generally. But the fundamentals are right and working.

On one of the last days of the Brand trial, I left the court building and recognized one of the jurors standing in front of me. They had their coat on, a bag on their shoulder, and were stepping out into a cold London evening totally anonymously. A thick gaggle of paparazzi, waiting for Russell and co., were lined up at the boundary. The juror slipped through without a single flashbulb going off, without a single paparazzo glancing or realizing this person might be someone worth photographing. That is exactly how it should be.

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Electric truck startup Harbinger has raised a $240 million Series D funding round as it expands its portfolio of products, including battery storage and defense applications.

The funding comes almost one year to the day after the Los Angeles-based company announced a $160 million Series C, which included an initial truck order from FedEx. Harbinger has now raised more than $650 million to date, according to Bloomberg News, which first reported the round.

The Series D was co-led by Tiger Global, Capricorn’s “technology impact fund,” and defense-focused firm Ridgeline. FedEx invested again after leading last year’s round, and other repeat investors included Maniv, Greycroft, and Leitmotif, a VC firm backed by Volkswagen. Also investing this time was institutional giant Fidelity Management, which tends to invest in pre-IPO startups. (Harbinger is reportedly weighing going public.)

While it didn’t lead this round, FedEx’s relationship with Harbinger continues to deepen. The shipping giant’s senior vice president of safety and transportation, Paul Melander, has joined Harbinger’s board of directors. FedEx also recently placed its first large order with Harbinger — a deal for 2,000 trucks valued at more than $300 million.

Founded in 2022 by former employees of now-defunct EV startup Canoo and battery company QuantumScape, Harbinger started by focusing on making one electric truck chassis. The company has steadily expanded its efforts since. It bought autonomy startup Phantom AI, has begun developing hybrid emergency vehicles, and revealed a driverless hybrid electric platform for the military.

Chainlink's new compliance framework uses CCIP, ACE and Data Streams to support onchain bond standards.

Shield AI to buy Bill Gates-backed radar maker Echodyne in $5.3B deal, doubling down on Seattle area

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

by Todd Bishop & John Cook on Nov 5, 2026 at 9:47 amNovember 5, 2026 at 10:41 am

Shield AI, the defense tech company planning to build its AI-piloted X-BAT fighter jet south of Seattle, agreed to acquire Kirkland, Wash.-based Echodyne, which makes compact radar systems that detect and track drones, in a transaction valued at up to $5.3 billion.

It’s a major exit for one of the Seattle region’s best-known deep-tech startups. Echodyne spun out of Intellectual Ventures, the Bellevue invention lab, in 2014 with a $15 million round led by Bill Gates and Madrona.

Echodyne, ranked No. 9 on the GeekWire 200, has raised about $200 million in total, and PitchBook put its valuation at $485 million after its last priced round in 2022.

The deal is believed to be the second-largest acquisition of a private venture-backed company in Washington state history, behind Okta’s $6.5 billion acquisition of Auth0 in 2021, Madrona’s Tim Porter wrote in a blog post. It’s among the top exits in the history of the Seattle-based venture firm, Porter said, noting that Echodyne is profitable.

To help pay for the acquisition, San Diego-based Shield said it’s raising $2.5 billion, including $1 billion in new funding at a $28.8 billion valuation. That’s more than double the $12.7 billion valuation it reached in March.

What Echodyne does: Echodyne’s radar is built into hundreds of counter-drone and security systems used in more than 40 countries, according to the companies. It’s used in remote weapon stations, high-energy lasers and microwave weapons, and it helps point interceptors at incoming drones.

Stadiums and airports use the same technology to spot drones that aren’t supposed to be there. Shield, best known for its Hivemind autonomy software and V-BAT drone, has served only government end users so far, CEO Gary Steele told Bloomberg.

Steele called Echodyne the company’s first step into hardware with civilian as well as military uses. “Think about public safety — whether it’s the Super Bowl or FIFA, just literally every single thing is going to need protection from drones,” he said.

Impact on jobs: Echodyne has about 400 employees, up from 260 when it opened a $40 million manufacturing facility in Woodinville, Wash., in July. No layoffs are planned, and Echodyne employees won’t be required to relocate, a Shield spokesperson said.

The combined company will have about 2,700 employees. Eben Frankenberg, Echodyne’s co-founder and CEO, will remain in that role, reporting directly to Steele, and will stay in charge of its products and manufacturing as well as its relationships with customers and partners.

Madrona invested $5.5 million in Echodyne’s initial $15 million round, at a $24 million pre-money valuation, and Porter joined the board. Madrona and other Echodyne investors will become significant Shield shareholders, according to the venture firm.

Echodyne makes all of its radar systems in the Seattle region, and Shield will continue to double down on that growth along with its investment in X-BAT manufacturing, Porter said via email.

“Seattle as a defense tech hub is real,” he said.

Shield said in September that it expects to employ about 4,000 people in the Seattle region by the early 2030s as X-BAT production ramps up at its site in Des Moines, Wash. The company projects the program will support about 8,000 jobs in the region in that time, including at suppliers.

The deal is Shield’s fifth acquisition and its largest. The others were Heron Systems and Martin UAV in 2021, Sentient Vision Systems in 2024 and Aechelon Technology in June.

The acquisition is expected to close in early 2027, pending regulatory approvals. Shield will pay $4.8 billion at closing, with up to $500 million more if Echodyne hits performance targets, and the $2.5 billion financing won’t go through if the deal falls apart, according to the companies.

Dartmouth and Onyx roots: Frankenberg has “a unique knack for getting brilliant people to believe in a vision, even when there’s plenty of skepticism around them,” said Brent Frei, the Smartsheet co-founder, who met Frankenberg as a freshman at Dartmouth and later roomed with him.

“We were a little bit like the Odd Couple; he was the consummate gentleman from North Carolina: well-mannered, organized, focused and very smart,” Frei said. “By contrast, I was the other character.”

Frei founded Onyx Software in 1994 and built it largely with a group of Dartmouth friends, including Frankenberg. Mark Mader, another of the group, later built Smartsheet with Frei, and Rob Eleveld went on to sell Ekata to Mastercard for more than $800 million.

“Our running joke is that we keep building 10-to-12-year overnight successes,” Frei said.

Matt McIlwain, a Madrona managing director who also goes back to Dartmouth with Frankenberg, called the Onyx “family tree” a “great example of how the innovation ecosystem and flywheel gets built over decades in a community like Seattle.”

Go hands-on with new launches the same week they’re announced, and hear directly from the engineers who built them. Experience the depth you won’t find anywhere else.
