No sweat: High-tech T-shirt brand Neat survives ‘Shark Tank’ and lands $400K deal with Lori Greiner

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Chronicling the Seattle and Pacific Northwest startup scene. SEE MORE

by Kurt Schlosser on Nov 5, 2026 at 9:45 amNovember 5, 2026 at 9:45 am

If Jim Huffman and Tyler Stelter were sweating during their pitch on ABC’s “Shark Tank,” their T-shirts didn’t show it. It probably helped them land a deal.

Huffman is CEO and Stelter is COO of Seattle-based Neat, which makes shirts designed to hide sweat. They survived a grilling from the Sharks on the popular reality TV series and secured an investment from Lori Greiner on Wednesday night’s episode.

Greiner agreed to invest $400,000 for a 15% stake in the company, plus 75 cents per order until she earns back her investment. Huffman said the deal is still being finalized.

“I love the product. I’m gonna be a huge customer,” Greiner, an inventor and longtime QVC personality, said on the show. “I already have 10,000 ideas of videos we’re gonna do.”

“It felt like a business knife fight,” Huffman told GeekWire on Thursday. The most intense part, he said, was negotiating with several Sharks at once while doing revenue-share math in his head.

Neat sells T-shirts and other tops lined with a patented “Sweat Maze,” a pattern of water-repelling and water-attracting zones that pulls moisture inward and spreads it across the inside of the shirt so it dries before it shows through.

The technology came out of research funded by the National Science Foundation and was originally developed for military personnel, firefighters and first responders.

“Shark Tank,” now in its 18th season on ABC, features entrepreneurs pitching their businesses to a panel of wealthy investors, known as Sharks, in hopes of landing an investment in exchange for a stake in their company. The show is based on “Dragons’ Den,” a Japanese format, and has won five Emmy Awards.

Joining Greiner on Neat’s panel were Kendra Scott, founder of the jewelry brand that bears her name; Kind snack bar founder Daniel Lubetzky; investor Kevin O’Leary; and Rashaun Williams, a venture capitalist and chief investment officer of Harbinger Sports Partners.

Huffman applied for the show on a whim, filling out an online form late one night. About 10,000 companies apply each season and roughly 70 make it on air, he said producers told him.

Huffman and Stelter filmed their pitch in June at Tyler Perry Studios in Atlanta. Taping there in the summer made it “the ultimate test” for the shirt, said Huffman, who added that he runs hot when presenting, even without studio lights.

Huffman and Stelter came into the tank asking for $400,000 for 5% of Neat, valuing the company at $8 million. O’Leary was so put off by the valuation that he said a lightning bolt should “come through the ceiling and fry you both.” He then dropped out.

O’Leary and Williams saw Neat as a niche product, Huffman said. But Scott and Lubetzky both made offers, and Lubetzky’s interest caught the Neat team off guard. “We weren’t ready for that!” Huffman said.

When O’Leary argued that the shirts only appealed to men, Scott countered that women sweat, too. “Have you heard of a hot flash, darling? Because it’s a real thing,” she said.

Scott offered $400,000 for 20%, saying the product “has got licensing power, but your valuation is nuts.” Lubetzky offered the same amount for 25% before pulling out. Greiner then came in at 15% plus the per-order royalty, and the Neat team took her deal.

“We’ve never built an eight- or nine-figure brand, and Lori has, so bringing in someone with that experience and knowledge is extremely exciting,” Huffman said.

Huffman and Stelter began working on the brand in 2024. Huffman also runs GrowthHit, a Seattle-based growth marketing agency he founded in 2016.

Huffman came across Neat through a GrowthHit client, a sports performance brand whose team had developed the technology. Having grown up in Oklahoma and lived in Texas and New York, Huffman said he knew the pain of hot days when you can “never wear heather gray.” When the client sent him a shirt, his reaction was, “Oh my gosh, this is amazing!”

GrowthHit had been looking for a brand of its own after earlier attempts to launch products didn’t pan out, Huffman said. The agency took over Neat, with the original team continuing to handle product development while GrowthHit ran branding and marketing.

Neat’s net sales grew more than 300% in 2025, to about $700,000, and the company is on track for $1.5 million to $2 million in sales this year, Huffman said.

Huffman credits the growth to basic growth-marketing moves. Neat positioned itself as something other than a regular cotton tee, pushed bundles over single shirts, upgraded its product photos and raised prices.

Neat’s newest shirt, the Sweat Defender Crew, sells for $59.99, with discounts for bundles. Huffman told the Sharks that each shirt costs about $11.70 to make.

The company scaled through Instagram and Facebook ads, going from about $20 a day in ad spend to roughly $2,000 a day.

Its best-known ad pops a water balloon inside a shirt to show the outside staying dry. “We need to be able to demonstrate the product in five seconds, or you’re toast,” Huffman said.

Neat is profitable and hasn’t raised venture funding, Huffman said. He said the company is on track for a 15% profit margin this year.

The company has four employees, two in Seattle and two in Portland. Its shirts are made in Vietnam and sold directly to consumers. They’re not on Amazon or in stores yet, but Huffman said “Shark Tank” could open new sales channels.

Traffic to Neat’s site jumped about 20-fold from the East Coast and Central time zones as soon as the segment aired, Huffman said. The company did more sales during the nine-minute segment than it does on a strong holiday, with a second wave when the show aired on the West Coast.

The most popular color since the show aired is the sage green Huffman and Stelter wore in the tank, which is also the color Neat has the least of in stock.

“Our main priorities right now are making sure the site doesn’t crash and that customer service can keep up,” Huffman said.

Virgil Van Dijk issues verdict on Liverpool's defensive inconsistency

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

The Reds have conceded three goals in each of their last three European outings, with their captain admitting the defensive shortcomings must be addressed

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Virgil van Dijk has conceded that Liverpool’s ongoing defensive problems in the Champions League cannot continue following a difficult evening in Istanbul.

Conceding three goals for the third consecutive match in Europe – an unprecedented occurrence in the club’s history – Andoni Iraola’s side were punished by Fenerbahce, having previously managed to win their prior two fixtures despite similar defensive lapses.

Particularly troubling in the 3-2 loss was not merely surrendering a lead on two occasions, but the complete loss of composure shown by the team after Fenerbahce scored their opener.

While the intimidating home crowd played a role in the collapse, Liverpool’s wider defensive record across the campaign has exposed deeper vulnerabilities.

Across 15 fixtures in all competitions, the team have recorded just four clean sheets – against Ipswich, Fulham, Bournemouth and Brighton – with only one coming in their past 10 matches.

Although their qualification prospects remain intact with four group games remaining, it represents a troubling pattern that requires immediate rectification.

"Definitely not sustainable," said the Liverpool captain.

"That’s why it is a very disappointing night for many reasons, but definitely the goals we conceded and the manner we conceded them as well. There is work to do.

"Three set-pieces as well. We have been very good at them so far this season but we switched off and were not ready for the first and second balls. It is a tough one to take."

Signs of frustration were visible even from the typically calm Dutchman, who shoved Irfan Kahveci to the ground during a second-half corner.

When asked whether he felt angry, Van Dijk added: "Yes. But I didn’t show it today because everyone looked disappointed and they have a reason to be, they should be disappointed.

Having suffered consecutive defeats for the first time under Iraola following a five-match winning run, focus must now shift to Sunday’s trip to struggling Crystal Palace to prevent further loss of momentum.

"There is always noise and people say or have opinions about us all the time. Our focus is only on improving," said the centre-back.

"We have been very good at defending set-pieces but (against Fenerbahce) we haven’t. That’s something we have to do better.

"This is a difficult one to take but we have to go to focus on Sunday. For our supporters it is just a shame, I feel sorry for all of them.

"We as a group have to go through good moments but also lesser good moments. That will only form a bigger bond, it will be only beneficial.

"No-one wants to lose games, especially how we did, but it should only help us in the near future. We should not accept losing especially in the manner we did."

Booked! SAS Business Class To Germany, Because My Wife Chose Horses Over The Caribbean

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

One beauty of sitting on all these SAS EuroBonus miles is that spur-of-the-moment trips are possible…like a trip to Germany for a few days.

My wife works very hard and rather unexpectedly was given five days, though without a lot of notice. Life is busy for all of us and of course the kids are in school, but as they quickly grow up Heidi and I have elected to take advantage of opportunities like this…for we will regret not doing so later.

Heidi initially suggested a “trip to the Caribbean…somewhere warm” and I thought it would be a good opportunity  to visit Saint Kitts and Nevis and check out the Park Hyatt there. I also suggested we could fly to Iceland with no set plan, get in the car, and drive. Wouldn’t that be fun? I’ve never done a trip around Iceland’s Ring Road.

Ultimately, though, Heidi spoke to her best friend in Germany and decided it would be a prefect opportunity to see her parents (remember, she did not join us on the summer trip) and ride horses with her friend in the Black Forest. I loved the idea and got to work on the flights.

There wasn’t business class from Los Angeles, but there was from Chicago exactly on our dates…five days, with four nights on the ground. Hardly long enough, but better than nothing? We’ll fly into Zurich, but I bought a separate ticket (on Norwegian!) to return from Basel to Copenhagen to catch the SAS connection to ORD…with a very tight connection.

So as I conclude my India Trip Report and move on to our summer trip to Europe, I’ll be sharing updates from this trip. We’ll be traveling in business class on the SAS A330, a product I’ve reviewed many times, though I may do so again if the situation warrants.

There’s never an ideal time to get away, but I really hope this will be a refreshing time for Heidi and indeed for all of us. And yes, even though it’s hardly something new or different, I get really excited ahead of an intercontinental trip, especially in a premium cabin.

The 2026 Political Ad Outlay Was the Dumbest Money Spent In the History of Advertising

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

2026 political advertising was a disgrace.

Campaigns torched billions on placements that had little-to-no impact on voter behaviors. Many ads backfired, and in a few cases — such as Texas and Ohio — did so spectacularly.

AdImpact says 2026 was the most expensive election year in history (including both presidential and midterms cycles), with $12 billion spent on ads. Most of that money was backed Republicans, but the GOP wipeout makes you wonder if it had any effect. Could things have gone worse for the party if campaigns and PACs had run no ads at all? Doubtful.

The most egregious examples are truly embarrassing for the losing campaigns. In Texas, Elon Musk’s over-the-top spending to back Trump loyalist Ken Paxton created an opening for James Talarico to surge on a “Texas is not for sale” message.

In Ohio, Vivek Ramaswamy’s desperate and gross ad featuring Amy Acton’s mother may go down as the worst miscalculation in political advertising history.

It wasn’t just Republicans who hurt their chances with terrible ad strategies. In Maine, Janet Mills had to bow out of the Dem primary after negative ads targeting Graham Platner backfired and her support dried up.

Beyond that, a whole bunch of deceptive and undisclosed AI-generated ads triggered negative media coverage and may have run afoul of the Federal Elections Committee.

It’s not that political ads never work, but they can’t accomplish what many campaigns seem to want them to do.

Specifically, ad spending will never overcome a broad-based shift in voter sentiment of the kind we just saw. Republicans outspent Democrats by $200 million in Texas, by $100 million in Ohio, by $84 million in Michigan. If such princely sums were spent to promote snacks or mobile games rather than political brands, the results would be real and highly measurable. Where’s the return on all this campaign spending? Nowhere to be found. Turns out, no amount of money could sway angry Texas voters to back Ken Paxton. When people have turned their back on a whole product category — whether MAGA-style politics or sugary drinks — the individual products and brands in that category stand no chance.

Also, 2026 campaigns vastly overrelied on a last minute onslaught of ads to change minds. Relentless October spending just dials up the saturation, inviting voter annoyance or outright hostility. The very best use of political advertising may be to build awareness for a candidate early in the cycle. Once that opportunity has been squandered, all that remains is to browbeat voters who already know (or think they know) your candidate. This is probably more true in a midterm race, when voters are on the whole more informed than in general elections.

Finally, please stay away from AI-generated creative — especially if it’s meant to deceive or confuse. The use of AI in political ads dramatically ramped up over the last two years, from $50,000 to at least $137 million. According to The Wesleyan Media Project, such ads are heavily skewed toward Republican campaigns. Fully 81% of AI-created ads supported GOP candidates and issues, compared with just 12% of Democrats. This makes some sense, since voters on the left generally have been louder in their disapproval of AI. But the public ire toward AI is increasingly bipartisan. The most telling thing about AI-generated ads is how many of them exclude disclosures — suggesting campaigns either know they’re unpopular, are deliberately misleading voters, or both.

No lie: The 2026 political ad outlay may have been the dumbest money spent in the history of advertising. Even so, media and tech platforms will be glad to take political money from the same undiscriminating sources again in two years’ time. It’s kind of a travesty.

The good news is, our relentless politics means campaigns will have the chance to mend their ways all too soon. I’d urge you to think like a smart commercial marketer: Find brands that are worth promoting, build the story early and learn from the mistakes of the past.

Zach Rodgers is a journalist, editor and podcast producer/host focused on the digital media and advertising industries.

Secure borders. A stronger economy. Washington must deliver both

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The editorial calls for immigration enforcement that protects due process and lawful work, plus modernized pathways aligned to construction labor needs. It points to Harvard and NAHB data and urges homebuilding leaders to press for measurable policy outcomes on affordability and productivity.  AI Summary

In the first 100 days of the Trump administration, business leaders at residential construction and development companies were, in effect, summoned to the nation’s capital for an all-hands meeting with administration officials on housing affordability. The summons came on short notice, on a Sunday afternoon. The invitation carried an undertone. Attend or else.

The Washington, D.C., meeting never took place. But the episode captured the breakneck pace and take-no-prisoners approach of those early, flood-the-zone months. It also dialed in a distinctly unsettling tone for the rules of engagement between the new administration and the nation’s private-sector business leaders in one of the economy’s critical engines: homebuilding.

Now, following the 2026 midterm elections, those rules of engagement stand an opportunity to change.

Business leaders, including corporate strategists and their representatives on Capitol Hill, have a window to act quickly, elevate their priorities, and press for policy changes that have damaged or threaten to undermine some of the nation’s most important economic capabilities.

For homebuilding, residential development, and residential investment, immigration enforcement belongs near the top of the agenda.

The United States needs secure borders and enforceable immigration laws. It also needs the workforce capacity to build homes, grow food, manufacture products, and sustain economic growth. These are not competing national interests, and treating them as such has real economic consequences.

The question for President Trump, the incoming Congress, and the business leaders with a stake in the outcome is whether the next two years can achieve a more rational, workable balance among those objectives.

For the housing industry, evidence of what’s at risk is already accumulating.

Over recent months, HousingWire TBD has documented the increasingly disruptive effects of immigration enforcement on residential construction businesses and their trade partners.

Builders and contractors report job site interruptions, workers reluctant to report for work, and crews disappearing from construction schedules. In some affected markets, executives have reported reductions of 60% to 80% in available trade crews. These are accounts from specific businesses and markets, not nationwide estimates.

The disruption also extends beyond workers who lack legal authorization. Reports that U.S. citizens and legally authorized workers are apprehensive about reporting to job sites suggest that enforcement-related fear can diminish workforce availability and productivity.

Residential construction depends on the coordinated sequencing of specialized trades. When a framing crew is unavailable, subsequent work may be delayed, schedules are extended, financing costs accumulate, and closings slip.

For builders and their trade partners operating with already compressed margins and limited capital flexibility, repeated disruptions can pose a serious business risk.

These accounts do not establish that immigration enforcement has caused a nationwide construction crisis. They do, however, raise a question policymakers cannot responsibly ignore: Are enforcement practices achieving legitimate security objectives while imposing avoidable economic costs on lawful workers, responsible employers, and American households?

A study published on October 8 by Harvard University’s Joint Center for Housing Studies found that foreign-born workers accounted for 35% of the nation’s construction trades workforce in 2024, compared with 18% of the overall U.S. labor force.

Their representation is especially high in trades critical to home completion: 57% of drywall and ceiling-tile installers, 53% of roofers and painters, and 51% of flooring installers were foreign-born.

Harvard’s research found that foreign-born workers accounted for all net growth in the construction trades workforce from 2013 to 2024.

Separate NAHB research found that Hispanic workers represented 32.8% of the construction workforce in 2024, up from 23.6% in 2010. In Texas and California, approximately six in 10 construction workers are Hispanic.

Foreign-born status, Hispanic ethnicity, citizenship, and legal employment authorization are distinct characteristics. These statistics cannot be used interchangeably to describe immigration status. Nor do they, nor can they, truly reflect the full impact of foreign-born workers on job site capability and productivity because many undocumented frontline crew members are not counted at all.

The statistics, however, illustrate the industry’s substantial dependence on a diverse workforce, whose lawful participation must be protected.

Meanwhile, labor shortages are worsening despite reduced housing production. NAHB’s September 2026 survey found that, averaged across nine consistently tracked trades, 47% of builders reported labor shortages, up from 42% in April 2025. Framing shortages reached 57%, while finished carpentry shortages reached 58%.

NAHB also identified immigration-enforcement-related worker apprehensions as a contributor to worsening shortages, along with competition from other construction sectors.

Immigration enforcement did not create the industry’s longstanding workforce problems. Those problems reflect decades of insufficient domestic workforce development, shifting demographics, competition for skilled labor, and limited productivity gains. But making an already constrained workforce even less available cannot be a rational part of a national housing affordability strategy.

Washington does not need to start from scratch.

Bipartisan immigration reform initiatives have repeatedly recognized the need to combine effective border security, lawful employment pathways, immigration-system modernization, and solutions for established workers and employers.

NAHB’s comprehensive immigration reform policy, updated in November 2025, provides a practical foundation for the industry. It supports border security and effective enforcement, along with construction-specific visa reforms, legal workforce pathways, and domestic workforce development.

The association has also endorsed the bipartisan DIGNITY Act, which would strengthen border security while establishing conditional pathways to lawful employment for certain undocumented workers who meet defined requirements.

These proposals involve difficult choices and legitimate disagreements. They show that practical approaches exist beyond the familiar binary choice between unrestricted enforcement and abandoning enforcement altogether.

The remaining weeks of the current Congress and the next two years should be evaluated against three governing objectives.

First, the administration must enforce immigration laws while protecting lawful employment and upholding due process. Enforcement priorities, worker-identification procedures, and accountability mechanisms should minimize unnecessary disruption to legitimate businesses and protect citizens and authorized workers from improper detention or interference.

Second, Congress and the administration must develop lawful workforce pathways that reflect documented economic needs. That means considering construction-specific employment programs, modernizing immigration processing, and addressing established workers’ circumstances through clear eligibility, screening, compliance, and worker-protection requirements.

Third, immigration policymaking must elevate economic productivity and affordability as measurable priorities. Congress should assess workforce availability, construction delays, business costs and housing production alongside border-security and enforcement outcomes.

None of these objectives diminishes employers’ obligation to comply with the law, protect workers, invest in apprenticeships and technical education, or to improve productivity through innovation.

America needs stronger domestic workforce development and a workable immigration policy, not one in place of the other.

The industry’s responsibility cannot end with documenting the disruption and asking Washington for relief. Homebuilders, residential developers, investors, building-products manufacturers, trade contractors, and their associations have the evidence, economic standing, and organizational capacity to demand a coherent policy response.

NAHB has already established a reform framework and identified legislative priorities. Industry executives have documented the operational consequences, and research institutions have quantified the workforce’s dependence on immigration.

What, then, are business leaders waiting for?

The next step should be a coordinated, industry-wide agenda presented to the administration and congressional leadership, including specific legislative priorities, credible evidence of business impacts, and commitments to lawful employment, investment in the domestic workforce, and improved productivity.

Not another statement of concern. Not another round of partisan accusations. A sustained effort to translate widely acknowledged economic needs into workable policy.

The new political balance in Washington creates an opening, but divided government does not automatically lead to better governance. Progress will require leaders willing to negotiate, make difficult choices, and accept accountability for results.

The homebuilding industry should not passively wait to see whether that happens. Its leaders have both a direct business interest and a broader responsibility to help make it possible.

America should not have to choose between secure borders and a productive economy. President Trump and Congress should be held accountable for delivering on both fronts.

Not only should the homebuilding industry insist that it do so, but the industry’s leaders should strike with the same speed and ferocity the Administration used to kick-start its agenda almost two years ago.

Celebrity Deaths of 2026: Kimmarie Johnson and More Stars We’ve Lost

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Hollywood mourned the deaths of some of its most legendary stars in 2026.

In June, legendary music executive Clive Davis also died at age 94 following a hospitalization for an upper respiratory infection.

In August, news broke that The Sopranos alum Vincent Pastore and former The Challenge winner Tyler Duckworth died. Dolly Parton also passed on August 25, one day before Tim Curry‘s death.

In September, Disney Channel star Carla Jeffery, legendary activist Gloria Steinem and jazz singer Cassandra Wilson died at the respective ages of 33, 92 and 70. Later that month, model Presley Gerber died at the age of 27.

One month later, news broke that former Big Brother contestant Amber Siyavus died at the age of 46. Breaking Bad alum RJ Mitte died at age 34 in October, while legendary actor Patrick Stewart died at age 86 following a battle with Alzheimer’s disease and Lewy body dementia.

November began with country singers Nika and Natalie Taylor announcing the death of their sister, Nicole Taylor. The Lettermen’s Tony Butala also died on November 1 at age 87, while Beverly Hills, 90210, alum Kimmarie Johnson died at age 50 on November 4.

Stewart was also remembered for his "many contributions to drama and the arts" by Marvel Studios. (Stewart filmed scenes as Professor X for the studio's upcoming Avengers: Doomsday, which premieres in December.)

"Sir Patrick Stewart leaves behind an indelible cultural footprint and legacy," the Disney-owned company said in a statement. "Marvel Studios extends its deepest condolences to his family, friends, and fans around the world."

Walter Koenig, who played Pavel Chekov in the original Star Trek series and seven Star Trek feature films, died on October 25 in Los Angeles, his rep confirmed.  “Today we mourn the passing of Walter Koenig, whose talent, warmth, and unforgettable performances left a lasting mark on generations of fans around the world. He passed of old age at home in North Hollywood last night surrounded by his daughter and niece,” his rep said in a statement.  The statement continued, “Best known for his iconic role as Pavel Chekov in ‘Star Trek: The Original Series,’ Walter helped inspire audiences with his optimism, humor, and adventurous spirit. Beyond ‘Star Trek,’ his long and distinguished career showcased his versatility as an actor and his genuine love for his craft. His remarkable talent continued to shine in his later years, most recently with his performance in the short film ‘Mr. Fleischmann,’ for which he received the Platinum Award for Best Actor at the 2026 Independent Shorts Awards — a fitting recognition of a lifetime devoted to his craft.” The statement also noted Koenig’s “kindness, wit and appreciation with everyone fortunate enough to meet him.” “He understood what Star Trek meant to people and embraced the community that grew around it,” the statement concluded. “On behalf of all of us, we extend our deepest condolences to Walter’s family, friends, colleagues, and the millions of fans whose lives he touched over the decades. Thank you, Walter, for the memories, the inspiration, and for boldly going where few have gone before.”

Recalling his time on Breaking Bad, the statement continued, “Drawing from his own experience living with cerebral palsy, Mitte brought authenticity to the role and built a platform on disability awareness and inclusion.” (Mitte was diagnosed with cerebral palsy at age 3.) “Beyond entertaining friends and family around the world, he served as an ambassador for United Cerebral Palsy and Shriners Children’s,” his rep added. “He also served as president of The Mitte Foundation, supporting philanthropic efforts and community initiatives, while using his platform to challenge stereotypes and promote greater representation for people with disabilities.” The statement concluded, “At this time, we ask people to respect the privacy of RJ Mitte’s family and close ones."

No cause of death was immediately disclosed. “It’s a tragic loss,” his spokesperson told Us. “Everyone loved Mr. Belding and those of us who got to know Dennis, loved him even more.” The statement concluded, “[Dennis] was a great person, and he loved, I mean absolutely LOVED his fans. The longtime NHL coach and ESPN analyst’s death at age 70 was announced on September 9. Melrose retired as one of ESPN’s color commentators in October 2023 after being diagnosed with Parkinson’s Disease. “From the flowing locks of his distinctive mullet to the flared lapels of his trademark long suit coats, Barry Melrose cut an unmistakable figure whenever you were fortunate enough to run into him at a rink or catch him on your television screen," NHL commissioner Gary Bettman said in a statement. "You were even more fortunate if you engaged him in conversation or listened to his analysis because he never failed to boost your love of the game of hockey." ESPN also paid tribute via a statement, which read, “For decades, Barry Melrose was one of the most distinctive and trusted voices in hockey. His knowledge of the game was deep, his candor was refreshing, and his passion was unmistakable. He was a foundational part of ESPN's hockey coverage, and fans and teammates truly appreciated his entertaining delivery and charismatic personality. "We send our deepest condolences to his loved ones. He always kept it interesting and he will be missed,” a network spokesperson added.

With a seven-decade career, she inspired multiple generations of artists and fans with her music and an unwavering commitment to making the world a better place. Her songs will continue to resonate with people of all ages, and her philanthropic work will have a lasting impact.” Per Parton’s request, a “small, private service will be held for immediate family,” and her “fans and friends from around the world are encouraged to pay tribute to Dolly on social media and her website.” The statement concluded, “In lieu of flower, the family asks for donations to be made to Dolly Parton’s Imagination Library."

In Real Sport Clube’s tribute to Castro, the team remembered him as “far more than a player.” (Castro signed with the club in June and never got to play in an official match for them.) “He was a member of our family, leaving a mark on all those who had the privilege of knowing him, both on and off the pitch,” Real Sport Clube’s statement read. [Coach] James Morrison’s men will also wear black armbands,” the team confirmed.

News broke on August 16 that New York Yankees icon Tommy John died at age 83 after battling bladder cancer. "The New York Yankees mourn the passing of Tommy John, who was a bedrock of our rotation during his time in pinstripes and a longtime favorite of Yankees fans,” a statement from the team read. “While his incredible longevity and post-surgery achievements changed the fortunes and trajectory of countless athletes around the world, it also overshadowed his excellence as a pitcher, which included consecutive 20‑win seasons with the Yankees.”

Many Challenge cast members paid tribute to the late reality star following the announcement of his death. “Absolutely heartbroken to hear about Tyler. We shared something special being part of The Challenge family,” Brad Fiorenza wrote via Instagram. “I’ll always be grateful for the memories we shared; especially at this last event just a couple weeks ago. Tyler will be deeply missed by so many. May his amazing soul live on in the lives he’s touched. RIP my dear friend 🕊️🙏” The Challenge star Tina Barta shared her own memorial message, writing, “Some friendships are measured in years. Others are measured in memories, laughter, shared experiences and the way someone makes you feel when they are in the room. @themightyduckworth you are one of those special people. A true friend and confidant.💔… Tyler, thank you for being part of my story. I’m grateful our paths crossed in this crazy life and that I get to carry those memories with me. Rest peacefully, my darling friend 🕊️.”

Gigi revealed that Reggie had a myriad of health problems — including Lewy body dementia and Parkinson's disease— due to being exposed to the herbicide Agent Orange while serving in the U.S. military during the Vietnam War. He is best known for playing the shotgun-wielding hero Reggie in the Phantasm horror movies. The South African Football Players Union announced Mamelodi Sundowns and Bafana Bafana midfielder Jayden Adams' death on July 11, only a few weeks after he competed in the World Cup. No cause of death was immediately announced. “His passing is an immeasurable loss to his family, teammates, clubs, the football fraternity and the country at large,” the organization said in a statement. “Death has cruelly stolen one of our own. It has robbed our nation of a remarkable footballer, but it will never take away the legacy Jayden Adams leaves behind. We will forever remember his humanity, his extraordinary talent and the pride with which he represented South Africa.” “There are no words to describe the pain I’m feeling. 💔 “Rest in peace, my love,” Adams’ girlfriend Aqueelah Chloe Adendorf wrote via Instagram. “Thank you for every memory, every laugh, every hug, and every moment we shared. You were not only the love of my life but also my greatest supporter and my best friend." She went on, “A part of my heart left with you, and I will carry your love with me forever. Until we meet again, I’ll miss you every single day. Rest easy, my angel. I love you always and forever." FIFA president Gianni Infantino added in his own statement, “It is so incredibly sad to hear that South Africa midfielder Jayden Adams has passed away just weeks after featuring in his nation’s historic FIFA World Cup campaign. My thoughts and condolences, as well as those of everyone at FIFA and the global football community, are with his family, friends and teammates. The Bafana and Mamelodi Sundowns star will be sorely missed. May he rest in peace.”

The South African Football Players Union announced Mamelodi Sundowns and Bafana Bafana midfielder Jayden Adams' death on July 11, only a few weeks after he competed in the World Cup. No cause of death was immediately announced.

A cause of death has yet to be determined, though Nashville police clarified that “foul play” was not suspected. Keith’s childhood friend and collaborator BlocBoy JB initially posted a “broken heart” emoji via Instagram following the producer’s death. “Damn cuz you just hurt me bad,” BlocBoy JB later wrote.

Gay candidates won where it was supposed to be impossible

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

On Tuesday night, two things happened at once, and the country will be tempted to remember only one of them.

The first made me go quiet at my kitchen table. Chris Pappas won election to the U.S. Senate in New Hampshire, becoming the first openly gay man ever sent to that chamber, and part of the margin that handed Democrats the Senate. In Indiana, J.D. Ford, the first openly gay person ever elected to that stateâs legislature, unseated a Republican incumbent and is headed to the House. In Texas, of all places, James Talarico beat Ken Paxton to become the first Democrat to win statewide in more than thirty years, after a campaign in which anti-LGBTQ+ messaging was reportedly Paxtonâs single largest category of advertising, and in which the vice president of the United States stood on a stage and used âgayâ as a punchline against him. Talarico is straight, and he won anyway.

And on Long Island, in a district Donald Trump carried, a gay Democrat named Chris Gallant, whose private photographs were leaked against him precisely because someone believed that seeing him would be enough to end him, came within about two points of flipping a seat the forecasters had rated safely Republican. He conceded the election on Wednesday, down by roughly 6,700 votes in the closest race of the incumbent Republican Nick LaLotaâs career. In the same breath, Gallant called LaLota a brother in arms and told the district he was still their neighbor, which is to say he refused the shame he was supposed to feel from the photo leak to the very end. Whatever that is, it is not the story of a man sunk by leather. It is the story of a man who nearly won the unwinnable while carrying the exact weight that was supposed to be disqualifying.

In 2026, you cannot reliably win an American election by making a manâs gayness, real or invented, the center of your campaign. Republicans tried it in Texas and lost the whole state. They tried it on Long Island and watched a red seat nearly flip.

Put those results next to one another and a sentence forms that would have been unimaginable a decade ago: In 2026, you cannot reliably win an American election by making a manâs gayness, real or invented, the center of your campaign. Republicans tried it in Texas and lost the whole state. They tried it on Long Island and watched a red seat nearly flip. The oldest weapon in the drawer â the one that raised me, the one that could empty a lunch table in the 1990s â is losing its edge at the ballot box. Voters keep looking at the openly gay candidate and sending him to Washington.

That is the headline, and it is real, and I am not going to let anyone talk me out of being moved by it.

But here is the second thing that happened Tuesday night. In the same hours, often on the same ballots, and it is the part the rainbow-wave coverage is already rushing past. In Arizona and Nebraska, voters approved measures to bar transgender girls from girlsâ sports. In Colorado, two measures aimed at transgender young people, one on sports and one restricting certain surgical care for minors, are narrowly passing. In Washington State, an initiative to keep transgender girls out of girlsâ sports passed comfortably. The same electorate that refused to punish a Senate candidate for being gay reached down the ballot and punished trans kids for existing.

This is the thing I need people to see before the celebration hardens into a story we tell ourselves. The contempt did not disappear on Tuesday. It relocated. A political movement that has learned it can no longer win by hating gay men has not given up the strategy. It has updated the target. The MAGA machine that aired the Paxton ads and laughed at Vanceâs joke is the same one now writing ballot measures about 11-year-olds, because cruelty still tests well when it is aimed at the group with the least power to answer back. Gays are simply the part of the community that got powerful enough to be left alone.

I know this pattern in my body, because I have watched it my whole life. The fear moves. First it was the plague, then it was the gay man as predator, then it was marriage as the end of the world, and each time the broader culture eventually got bored or ashamed and the fear went looking for a new address. Right now the address is a trans child in a locker room. The people who found that child did not arrive by accident. They are the same people, running the same play, because it is the only play they have ever had.

Want more sharp takes on politics? Sign up for our free newsletter, Standing Room Only, written by Amanda Marcotte, now also a weekly show on YouTube or wherever you get your podcasts.

So I want to be careful about the victory lap, even as I take one. It is a genuine and hard-won thing that Chris Pappas will be a senator and J.D. Ford will be a congressman, and that a straight man in Texas could not be destroyed by being called gay. Those wins are not small, and the people who did the work to make them real earned every bit of the joy.Â

But a movement does not get to accept its own seats at the table while the youngest and most defenseless members of its own community are voted off the field in four states on the same night. This is not a win we get to keep cleanly. It is a split screen, and if we only look at our half of it, we become exactly the kind of people who could be bought off with a little representation while someone else pays the bill.

The refusal to be ashamed won some rooms on Tuesday. I felt it. The task now is to extend that refusal to the people the machine has decided to hunt next, to say as loudly for trans kids what the voters of New Hampshire and Indiana and Texas just said for us: that you do not get to make a personâs existence a punchline, or a wedge, or a ballot measure. We learned that lesson about gay men the slow and brutal way. The whole point of learning it is to spare the next group from learning it alone.

Chris Gallant did not quite win. But he came close enough to prove the old weapon is dulling. The question Tuesday really asked is whether we will hand the people wielding it a new one, or finally take the thing out of their hands.

JonBenet Ramsey's Brother Burke Shares What He Thinks Will 'Solve' Cold Case

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

JonBenét Ramsey’s brother, Burke Ramsey, is not giving up on solving his late sibling’s murder.

“It’s the most tragic, personal, awful thing to happen in my life, that anybody could imagine happening to them, and it’s tough to have it come up every ten years or so,” Burke, 39, told the Daily Mail in an interview published Thursday, November 5. “It just brings all that pain back. But I want this solved.”

When he was 9 years old, Burke’s 6-year-old sister was found dead after being strangled in the basement of the family’s Colorado mansion on December 26, 1996.  An autopsy later determined that she died from asphyxiation by strangulation and a skull fracture. The autopsy report also revealed acute physical and sexual trauma sustained at the time of her death.

It ultimately took 12 years for the Boulder Police Department to officially exonerate JonBenét’s family from any wrongdoing.

According to Burke, the key to solving the cold case may be mystery DNA found on JonBenét’s underwear and beneath her fingernails that pointed to an “unknown male intruder.”

“We need to find that match. It’s the best evidence we have. It was underneath her fingernails, and it didn’t match any of us,” Burke told the publication. “It’s probably the person who did it, and it’s the best chance we have to solve this. And I want it solved.”

He continued, “I don’t know much about the scientific nitty-gritty, and it’s hard to learn the details of the case because it is emotionally taxing. But they need to send those samples to different labs again and keep testing. Find the match. That will go a long way to bringing justice. It’s been 30 years and enough is enough.”

Burke’s rare interview comes as journalist Diane Sawyer takes a closer look at the case in JonBenét Ramsey: Race Against Time, Who Is ‘Unknown Male 1’? – A New Diane Sawyer Investigation, which premieres on ABC Friday, November 6.

Netflix is also preparing a new 8-part drama series called The Mother of JonBenét Ramsey, which features Melissa McCarthy playing Patsy Ramsey.

Nearly 30 years after JonBenét was murdered, Burke makes it clear his family was not responsible for any crime.

“The DNA says we didn’t do it. I know none of my family did it. I didn’t do it,” he shared. “It’s like, stop. There was this energy looking at the family, but the DNA is sitting right there.”

As another difficult anniversary approaches, Burke hopes followers of the case remember his sister for so much more than her experience in beauty pageants. After all, it was only “a small part of JonBenét’s life.”

“She had a lot more to her than that,” he said. “To me, she was just a normal sister who would slide down hills on boxes with me. We’d play in water together. Very normal stuff.”

Is Your Home Too Matchy-Matchy? These 5 Designer Tricks Break Up the Blah and Add Instant Character

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

If your bedroom design began with a furniture set that matches your nightstands and lamps, it may be time to shake things up. Having a space where everything coordinates perfectly may seem like the easiest route to a stylish home, but many times, it can feel more sterile than a true reflection of who you are and what you love.

“A home often looks sterile when everything comes from the same set,” explains Preeti Shah, founder and principal designer at Spotlight Style.

By stepping out of your comfort zone and switching things up a bit, you can create a cohesive look that still brings character and depth to your home. Here are five designer tips to steer you in the right direction.

Just because one of your surfaces is natural white oak doesn't mean everything in the same room needs to follow suit.

“We love mixing dark wood tones with light tones and everything in between,” says Leah Hook, founder and principal designer at Gray Oak Studio.

Repeating wood tones can make a room feel cohesive, but you don't need to use the same tone everywhere. Think outside the box and look for subtle ways to tie the space together, such as repeating a wood tone in an art frame or decorative tray.

Believe it or not, your island does not have to match your kitchen countertops in every possible way.

“Choose a more exciting stone for your island. You can even miter it two inches thick instead of keeping everything the standard 1.25-inch thickness,” explains Gaelle Dudley, founder and creative director at GLDESIGN.

Dining chairs are almost always purchased as a matching set, but sourcing different styles from flea markets or Facebook Marketplace can give your dining room a fun, unexpected look.

“Select just one element from the table that will be repeated in the chairs, and go with something from a different collection or even an entirely different furniture line,” says Pattie Kelly, certified interior decorator and owner of Inspired Home Interiors.

Consider the height of the chairs (so that they still make sense together at the table) and how much room you have to pull them out in (so you’re not running into the wall behind them).

The most interesting homes display a mix of different art styles. Instead of sticking to one color palette or aesthetic, seek out pieces that contrast. For instance, Shah is a fan of contrasting graphic black-and-white drawings with gold-veined abstract canvases in various rooms of the home.

Speaking of artwork, as the space pictured above by Nina Garbiras illustrates, don’t be afraid to mix and match your frame materials. Just keep one element consistent, like the mat size or the frame width, so that it still has a curated (not chaotic) feel.

Just because you’re choosing one type of fixture finish doesn’t mean you have to repeat it everywhere. In fact, your space will look more “expensive” if you match shiny finishes very minimally.

For example, the above space by Vestige Home showcases how well brass and matte-black hardware can play off each other, adding depth, harmony, and visual interest. Choosing to go with one finish throughout this space would’ve become too matchy-matchy very quickly.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

The Good, The Bad and The Ugly of Scott Parker’s Managerial Career

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

With Scott Parker now in the manager’s seat, supporters are naturally asking the same question: what exactly will a Parker team look like?

Last night gave us a glimpse of his ability to deliver a rousing half-time team talk, but what else can he bring to the table besides a penchant for pricey cardigans?

His managerial career has established a clear tactical identity, one that has delivered success in the Championship but has also attracted criticism at higher levels.

Gonzo has already covered this in a previous article, citing that Parker typically favours either a 4-2-3-1 or 4-3-3 formation, built around organisation, possession and defensive discipline.

His teams are usually constructed from the back, with an emphasis on retaining control of the ball rather than engaging in frenetic, end-to-end football. The objective is often to dominate territory through patient build-up play, moving possession into wide areas to stretch opponents before creating openings.

Defensively, Parker’s sides tend to be compact and difficult to break down. A double pivot in midfield frequently provides protection for the back line, allowing his teams to maintain their shape and reduce space between the lines.

The philosophy is clear: control games first, take risks second.

While that approach has often proved effective in the Championship, critics argue it can sometimes become overly cautious, particularly when chasing games or facing stronger opposition.

One aspect of Parker’s CV cannot be ignored: he knows how to get out of the Championship.

He guided Fulham to promotion through the play-offs in 2020 and followed that by leading Bournemouth to automatic promotion in 2022. His ability to organise teams over the course of a demanding Championship season is well established.

For a West Ham side whose only objective is a return to the Premier League, that experience was undoubtedly a major factor behind the club’s decision.

The bigger question is whether he can combine results with the attacking football supporters crave.

Will Parker get West Ham on the front foot?

At Fulham, supporters generally look back on Parker’s spell with a degree of respect.

Promotion to the Premier League remains a significant achievement, but frustration grew during the following campaign as the football became increasingly conservative. Despite periods of decent organisation, Fulham were ultimately relegated and some supporters felt the team’s cautious approach limited its potential.

With regards to his time at Bournemouth, Parker’s stock was initially high on the south coast after guiding the club back to the Premier League at the first attempt.

However, his relationship with supporters deteriorated rapidly following promotion. Public criticism of the club’s transfer business and squad depth, particularly after a heavy 9-0 defeat at Liverpool, created tensions that cost him his job.

His dismissal divided opinion, especially after Gary O’Neil kept Bournemouth comfortably in the Premier League with largely the same group of players.

Parker’s short tenure in Belgium at Club Brugge proved to be the most difficult period of his managerial career.

Expectations were high upon his arrival, but results and performances failed to follow. His tactical approach was widely criticised and he struggled to adapt quickly enough to the demands of Belgian football.

The experiment was brought to an abrupt end after only a brief spell in charge.

Burnley supporters largely viewed Parker’s appointment through a practical lens.

His track record of Championship promotions offered reassurance, but concerns remained over whether his style could provide the excitement and attacking intent fans wanted to see.

As with many clubs that have employed Parker, appreciation for results was often balanced against questions surrounding entertainment value.

That said, I actually know a Burnley fan, and when Parker was appointed, he messaged me just two words: “Good appointment!”

The challenge facing Parker at West Ham is clear. We arguably possess one of the strongest squads in the division and supporters will expect more than simply grinding out results. Promotion remains the priority, but fans will also want to see an identity return to the team after the uncertainty of the Nuno Espírito Santo era.

One thing is certain: after months of instability, West Ham have turned to a manager with a proven track record at this level.

Now comes the difficult part. Turning potential into winning games!

Now he’s in, let’s unite behind him and see where he can take us. COYI!

I have been a season ticket holder since the late eighties, so experienced the highs and lows of being a West Ham supporter. I previously wrote for OLAS and have contributed to a number of football publications in the past.

Our current goalkeeper has not laid a hand on the last nine goals he has let in. Virtually every corner if he makes a parry out, it is always at 90 degree angle to our goal, so making an assist for the opposition, who have gone on to score from this assist. Ex professional goalkeeper Nuno, could not help this boy change his bad habits, surely Scott has already realised changes start with the goalkeeper

Scott’s style of play will have to be adapted to utilise to the full the talents of this current squad.

This will be a totally bespoke solution to the formation conundrum. Scott is up to the task in hand. When he had gained promotion with the other three teams, they sold his best players and expected him to work miracles whilst remaining silent about the circumstances.

Considering the rug-pulling, Scott did a great job at each club. With some support to enhance and progress the playing staff available to him he would have been well established as a leading PL manager by now.

I still believe he has the attributes to be that leading PL manager and I hope that will be whilst in the employ of West Ham.

openSUSE Leap 16.1 gives Linux users the right to remain immutable

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

One installer now offers conventional and read-only systems as separate Micro distro bows out

openSUSE Leap 16.1 lets users choose between a conventional Linux installation and an immutable system from the same installer, bringing the previously separate Leap Micro into the fold.

It's the first point release after Leap 16.0, which we looked at last year when it reached the release candidate stage.

Leap 16.1 has arrived ahead of its paid enterprise sibling, SUSE Linux Enterprise Server 16.1, which is expected later this month. Its release notes are already available, and IBM discussed the release in September.

The big change in the 16.1 release cycle of both the free and commercial products is that SUSE is merging its conventional Linux with its immutable version, a change the company announced back in February.

SUSE previously offered a commercial stripped-down immutable OS, aimed mainly at hosting containers, called SLE Micro. To correspond with that, there was a free community OS called Leap Micro – the final release was Leap Micro 6.2.

Leap Micro is being discontinued as a separate community distribution.  The Leap 16.1 announcement says: "Immutable Mode with transactionally updating a system with a read-only root filesystem is a big change for this release. Users who know Leap Micro will recognize it right away, because it is the same approach, now integrated directly into Leap. Leap 16.1 in Immutable Mode replaces Leap Micro, and there will be no Leap Micro 6.3 or 7.0. Users simply boot the regular Leap 16.1 install image and select Immutable Mode in Agama."

Both Red Hat and Canonical offer immutable distros, but both use different architectures from their conventional package-based distros. Ubuntu Core is built entirely from snap packages, while Red Hat's "Atomic" immutable distros are built from bootable containers, running on top of OStree, which works a little like a local Git server. Immutable distros built on Arch, such as SteamOS 3 or KDE Linux, resemble ChromeOS at filesystem level, with dual root filesystems that update one another.

Leap belongs to the openSUSE family of distros, backed by enterprise Linux vendor SUSE. Its commercial sibling is SUSE Linux Enterprise Server (SLES). Leap is the free, community-supported version, and they are based on the same core packages and release schedule. openSUSE also has a rolling-release version, called Tumbleweed.

To rephrase this in terms of slightly more familiar Red Hat equivalents, SLES is SUSE's equivalent of RHEL. Leap is the free openSUSE equivalent of the late lamented CentOS Linux. Leap shares a common core with SUSE's enterprise products while offering a broader community package selection. SLES and openSUSE began sharing enterprise binaries in 2021. Red Hat, meanwhile, announced the end of CentOS Linux in 2020, shifting its focus to CentOS Stream.

To continue the Red Hat comparisons, openSUSE Tumbleweed is loosely equivalent to Fedora Rawhide, but with automated testing and rollback of problematic updates. Unlike the best-known rolling distro, the entirely community-built Arch Linux, Tumbleweed uses SUSE's Snapper technology to offer snapshots. If an update breaks something, Tumbleweed users can boot into a snapshot of an older version of the OS, before the offending update was installed, and keep on working. If dozens of updated packages a day prove too much, then openSUSE also offers an experimental slower-moving rolling release called Slowroll.

Despite the modest version-number bump, Leap 16.1 is a substantial annual update. Its release cycle sits between Fedora and interim Ubuntu releases on one side, and Debian and Ubuntu LTS on the other. Along with its other updates, it brings GNOME 48.8, KDE Plasma 6.6.4, and LXQt 2.4.

We tried its Xfce desktop, which surprisingly defaulted to X.org rather than the Wayland-based setup using labwc that we saw in 16.0 last year.

Immutable distros look to be the future of Linux for non-technical users. Android is an immutable OS based on the Linux kernel, and sells in the billions – which is why, as we reported last year, Google is replacing ChromeOS with Android. The result is the AI-festooned GooglebookOS, on sale in Mountain View's new high-end GoogleBook kit.

The Reg FOSS desk isn't aware of any other Linux OS in which you're able to choose, at the point of installation, whether you wish it to be immutable or not. For now, in the commercial Linux OS space, only SUSE has anything like this flexibility. It's marketing its immutable versions as being ideal hosts for containers, and as suitable for "edge" devices, meaning that they are expected to be provisioned remotely, update themselves automatically, and recover from faults on their own.

SUSE is a much smaller company than Red Hat, and unfortunately, as we reported earlier this week, it is getting smaller.

This vulture is both a former SUSE Linux user and a former SUSE employee, and we hope it and its unique distribution survive and thrive. ®

Opinion: Hey, That Hellraiser: Revival Game Is Pretty Good

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

For as much as I love the horror genre, I've never seen a Hellraiser film. If they're as fun to watch as Hellraiser: Revival is to play, though, then maybe I need to catch up.

Since we've got The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time and GTA 6 right around the corner (I'm now playing the former), I haven't wanted to start a big game that requires a serious time investment. With a 10-hour playthrough for the Platinum Trophy (which I did earn), this new Hellraiser game seemed like the perfect fit.

This is a straightforward, mostly linear survival horror game that gets in and out without overstaying its welcome. It shows touches of flair and ambition at some points, with light takes on the more open-ended Resident Evil approach to exploration, providing a little area to progress through. Those moments are cool, and in between them is action, puzzle-solving, horror, sex, and the Labyrinth.

You know me by now: I love survival horror and all the typical trappings of the genre. When it wants to — a few times during the campaign — Hellraiser: Revival does them adequately enough. It's no Resident Evil or Silent Hill killer, so what I've loved most about the game are the factors you'd stereotypically associate with the IP: chains, Pinhead, telekinesis, and the Labyrinth.

Main character Aidan and his girlfriend Sunny decide to use a Genesis Configuration cube during sex, but activating the thing calls upon Pinhead and his cronies, interrupting the intimacy (of which there is a lot). Sunny is taken hostage and Aidan has to fight back through guns, melee weapons, and the Genesis Configuration.

This elaborate puzzle box grants him telekinesis powers, and later, the ability to wield fireballs.

The mechanic is a standout, and presents a welcome alternative to stabbing and blasting guys and gals in gimp masks.

You can summon saw blades, spikes, and sharp debris and throw them at zombies and cultists. If you see a brazier, you can steal its fire and lob it at enemies. With the blaze it creates, you can create more fireballs from the burnt remains. It is a neat little system that I thoroughly enjoyed, and would love to see expanded should there ever be a sequel.

My favourite sequences overall, though, took place in the Labyrinth. In this alternate realm, you face spike traps, moving saws, contorting paths and staircases, and demons like the Chatterer that chase you. Also requiring you to move blocks with your telekinesis powers under a time pressure, these sequences are genuinely intense. You can feel the Chatterer stalking you as its teeth, well, chatter.

Is Hellraiser: Revival a hidden gem that should be claiming Game of the Year nominations? No.

Viewing the Metacritic page for a gauge of its critical reception at launch, I think an overall rating of 69 to 76, depending on what platform you're playing on, is bang on. If I were reviewing Hellraiser: Revival, with my critic cap on, I don't think I could score it any higher than a 7/10.

However, I've been playing Hellraiser: Revival for fun rather than my job, and that's exactly what I've had: fun.

I want to see more from this universe; I hope Boss Team Games gets a chance to develop a big sequel. If you're reading, publisher Saber Interactive, that's what I want.

In the meantime, I'll go and catch up on those movies.

Have you played Hellraiser: Revival since it launched for PS5 last month? What did you make of the game? Let me know if you agree with my opinion in the comments below.

Meta Secures $15B Senior Notes for General Corporate Purposes

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Meta Platforms completed a $15 billion, five-series senior notes offering on November 5, 2026, according to its current report filed with the SEC.

The final prospectus supplement, dated November 3, 2026, estimates net proceeds of approximately $14.9 billion after underwriting discounts and estimated offering expenses. Meta intends to use the proceeds for general corporate purposes. The five fixed-rate series have these final terms:

Interest is payable semiannually each May 15 and November 15, beginning May 15, 2027. Citigroup and Morgan Stanley served as joint book-running managers. The notes are unsecured and unsubordinated, rank equally with Meta’s other senior unsecured debt, carry no subsidiary guarantees and are structurally subordinate to subsidiary liabilities.

The prospectus’s pro forma presentation as of September 30, 2026 lists actual non-current long-term debt of $80.92 billion and an adjusted balance of $95.92 billion. Actual cash, cash equivalents and marketable securities were $82.45 billion, with an adjusted total of $97.39 billion. The adjusted figures reflect receipt of the estimated net proceeds before those proceeds are used.

Meta reported third-quarter capital expenditures, including principal payments on finance leases, of $34.91 billion in its third-quarter results, and narrowed its 2026 capital-expenditure outlook to $135–145 billion from $130–145 billion. Operating cash flow was $32.79 billion, while free cash flow was negative $2.12 billion. Meta defines free cash flow as operating cash flow less purchases of property and equipment and principal payments on finance leases, tying the measure directly to those capital outlays.

Meta said it expects to continue investing aggressively in its core business and emerging AI initiatives during 2027, with current and scheduled capacity providing flexibility to allocate resources across model training, core business growth, and the development and support of new products.

The current report also disclosed that underwriters fully exercised their 30-day option on November 4, 2026 for an additional 6 million Class A shares. Meta entered additional forward sale agreements with Morgan Stanley Capital Services LLC and Goldman Sachs & Co. LLC on essentially similar terms to the original agreements.

The original October 26, 2026 agreements covered 40 million Class A shares sold by forward sellers that borrowed the shares from third parties. Meta received no initial proceeds from those sales.

The additional Morgan Stanley confirmation and Goldman Sachs confirmation each cover 3 million shares, are dated November 4, 2026 and effective November 5, 2026, and set an initial forward price of $622.91875 per share.

Subject to conditions, Meta can select settlement dates through November 19, 2027. On settlement, it will issue shares to the forward purchasers and receive cash calculated from the applicable forward price, which adjusts daily using an overnight funding-rate formula and scheduled reductions. The additional confirmations use physical settlement.

The agreements also address Meta’s planned Class C shares. After those shares are issued and other conditions are satisfied, the agreements reference both Class A and Class C stock and allow settlement solely in Class C shares in specified circumstances. The amended and restated certificate of incorporation creating the Class C stock has an effective date of January 19, 2027. Meta’s board approved the new class for capital raising, employee compensation, potential acquisitions and other general corporate purposes.

The debt sale is completed funding with fixed interest obligations, while Meta’s cash receipts and new share issuance under the additional forward agreements depend on later settlement. That separates immediate debt financing from deferred equity-related funding as Meta continues an AI investment program that coincides with negative quarterly free cash flow.

Nintendo president Shuntaro Furukawa said during the company’s latest earnings call that it’s “not considering additional changes” to the Switch 2’s price, Reuters reports. The Switch 2 got a $50 price hike earlier this year, bringing its starting price up to $499. Nintendo said in Thursday’s earnings report that Switch 2 sales have dropped 37.4 percent year-over-year.

According to Reuters, Furukawa also gave an update on the RAM shortage, saying, “There has been no major change to the memory situation and Nintendo is in a position to secure sufficient quantities.”

Demand from AI data centers is driving the ongoing RAM shortages that’s leading to a wave of tech price increases. Microsoft has raised Xbox Series X / S prices multiple times now, increasing the Series S’s starting price from $299 to $499. The PS5 has also been hit with price hikes, taking its starting price up to $649.

Why Godzilla Minus Zero Is Rated R

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

This article contains light spoilers for "Godzilla Minus Zero."

For about as long as the Motion Picture Association (formerly "of America") has existed, the conventional wisdom has been that blockbuster genre movies should receive the most inviting rating possible. This concept has existed at least since Steven Spielberg's "Jaws" took the movie industry and pop culture by storm, as its PG rating allowed a wide audience to experience it. Certainly there have been exceptions, most famously at the hands of Spielberg himself, whose 1984 output revealed the necessity for a rating in between PG and R: the PG-13.

This middle ground rating then became the go-to standard for films that are edgy and adult without crossing a line into becoming too much for young teens. Its popularity has resulted in many filmmakers pushing its limits, especially as it came to be known as essentially the default movie rating. So far, every "Godzilla" film has received a rating of PG-13 or lower, as most of the movies involve giant monster-on-giant monster violence (thus meaning it's impossible to emulate in real life, as per the MPA's general view on violence).

However, that has now changed with the release of "Godzilla Minus Zero," which has been given an R rating by the MPA. At first blush, it might seem that this is the "Godzilla" franchise's move to appeal to an edgier audience, in the same way the Marvel Cinematic Universe was only too happy to make "Deadpool and Wolverine" a hard-R. Yet "Minus Zero" isn't R-rated merely for surface-level indulgences like harsh language, extreme violence, or even sex. Instead, "Godzilla Minus Zero" earns the R for two very specific reasons, both of which make the higher rating more understandable.

Ever since the very first "Godzilla" in 1954, the giant monster has been intentionally positioned as an allegory for the ravages of war — specifically, war with atomic weapons, whose devastation Japan had recently experienced. Though Ishirõ Honda's film is a much more thoughtful and somber affair than modern audiences would expect, it's still got enough B-movie flavor to explain why the franchise went on to become increasingly focused on spectacle and even more kid-friendly. While the darkness inherent in "Godzilla" has cropped up in several movies since, the allegorical nature of the character is usually kept to subtext.

Writer/director Takashi Yamazaki takes the franchise further in "Godzilla Minus Zero" by depicting real-life war violence: the immediate aftermath of the US bombing of Hiroshima. In the film, the fictional character Kanji Murakami (Min Tanaka) is one of the few survivors of the attack, and we're shown a flashback in which he finds the charred remains of his family.

The upsetting reality of this moment not only fuels the themes of the film, but directly informs Murakami's character, as we learn that his plan is to lure Godzilla to New York City and have the beast attack America. In this brief but powerful fantasy sequence, we see Godzilla shoot his heat rays at the city, causing people's faces to melt off before they incinerate. It's a sequence which does something rare, especially for blockbuster genre films: it depicts an intentional act of genocide on Americans. It's a moment reminiscent of something like "Terminator 2" (which Yamazaki was inspired by), yet even more pointed given the emotion behind it. As such, it makes sense that the MPA would consider it rougher material than previous "Godzilla" movies.

As many have pointed out during the MPA's 60-odd year existence, the organization's rationale for a film's rating is often frustratingly vague. Many filmmakers are annoyed by the process of submitting and resubmitting their movie to get their desired rating, with many decisions coming down to the tiniest omission of a handful of frames, as opposed to large swathes of material. While the MPA's rulings are made on a literal case-by-case basis, the general idea seems to be that they're usually judging a film by its overall tone and effect on an audience, rather than ticking checkboxes. While some elements like nudity and more than one F-bomb in the dialogue are near-guaranteed criteria for an R, some movies earn the rating through what the MPA likes to call "thematic elements."

As such, the R rating for "Godzilla Minus Zero" is more of a cumulative one than anything else. Even if the Hiroshima flashback and NYC dream sequence were edited out, the film might edge close to an R solely for its grisly fight sequences between Godzilla and King Ghidorah. Both kaiju are particularly vicious in the way they take advantage of their respective regenerative powers, with the monsters ripping off each other's heads and limbs, and we see sinewy tendons stretching before they snap. Although it's still fantasy monster violence, when the film as a whole is taken into account, it does feel like giving it a PG-13 would be inappropriate, too. Ultimately, the rating is a reflection of how strong Takashi Yamazaki's filmmaking is. It makes "Godzilla Minus Zero" the latest in a proud lineage of light R-rated films, meaning it's likely to become a gateway genre movie for generations to come.

"Godzilla Minus Zero" is in theaters now.

ChatGPT’s image generator has gotten even better

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

It seems OpenAI likes to boost its chatbot’s image-generation capabilities at a rapid pace.

Just two months ago, users were introduced to ChatGPT Images 2.5. That update brought faster photo generation, sharper image details, more precise editing, and improved tools for creating & sharing. And now that we’ve reached November, Images 2.5 has officially entered its 2.6 phase.

Images 2.6 also promises an even faster rate of photo generation. However, the most exciting aspect of this new update is being able to turn any prompt you can think of or any photo you upload into a GIF. Alongside that welcome feature is the ability to adjust the animation speed of that GIF, edit its individual frames and share the final result across your social media.
