Adi Hütter reacts to Younes Ebnoutalib’s inclusion and Jonathan Burkardt’s exclusion from Germany squad

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Eintracht Frankfurt head coach Adi Hütter has expressed mixed feelings about Jürgen Klopp’s latest Germany national-team selection. While striker Younes Ebnoutalib has retained his place in the 24-man squad for November’s Nations League fixtures against Serbia and the Netherlands, fellow Frankfurt forward Jonathan Burkardt and goalkeeper Noah Atubolu have missed out.

Speaking at his Thursday pre-match press conference, Hütter praised Ebnoutalib’s remarkable development while acknowledging the disappointment experienced by Burkardt. The Austrian also discussed the difficult selection decisions facing Klopp following the Bundestrainer’s decision to reduce his previous 44-man squad to just 24 players.

Ebnoutalib is one of five players who made their senior Germany debuts during the previous international window and have retained their places.

Ebnoutalib’s progression has been among the more remarkable stories in German football over the past two years. Having played Regionalliga football with FC Gießen as recently as early 2025, the Frankfurt-born attacker subsequently established himself at SV Elversberg before securing a January 2026 transfer to his hometown Bundesliga club.

The youngster scored on his Bundesliga debut against Borussia Dortmund, although an injury shortly afterward interrupted his first months at Eintracht Frankfurt. Ebnoutalib returned strongly during the summer, impressing Hütter during preseason before scoring a hat-trick in Frankfurt’s opening Bundesliga encounter against Union Berlin.

His early-season form subsequently earned him a place in Klopp’s first Germany squad. In Get German Football News’ extensive feature on Ebnoutalib’s first national-team call-up, the youngster revealed that he had been moved to tears following a personal telephone conversation with the Bundestrainer.

Hütter now acknowledges that even he has been surprised by the speed of Ebnoutalib’s progress.

“We noticed in summer training camp, the Pokal, and [the opening Bundesliga match against Union Berlin] what he’s capable of,” Hütter said of Ebnoutalib at his Thursday pre-match press conference. “I myself, in all honesty, am surprised by the pace of his development. Of course, I’m in a comfortable place with two strikers who regularly score goals. It’s important that Younes remains grounded. He does that and shows that every day in training.”

The two strikers have frequently provided Hütter with difficult selection decisions. As previously documented in GGFN‘s analysis of Frankfurt’s tactical changes, the Austrian’s decision to switch to a back-three system in September required him to sacrifice one attacker. Ebnoutalib consequently made way for Burkardt in the starting lineup against Mainz despite having already registered four Bundesliga goals and an assist.

Nevertheless, both players have continued to demonstrate their value to the Frankfurt attack. Ebnoutalib most recently scored the decisive third goal in Frankfurt’s 3-1 Bundesliga victory over Hamburger SV, benefiting from Can Uzun’s excellent midfield ball recovery and subsequent through ball.

While Hütter welcomes Ebnoutalib’s continued involvement with Germany, the Frankfurt trainer is less pleased that Burkardt has been excluded. The 25-year-old has enjoyed a productive start to the Bundesliga campaign after overcoming the injury problems that disrupted his previous season.

Burkardt’s importance to Frankfurt was already evident during preseason. The former Mainz striker appeared particularly well suited to Hütter’s attacking approach, combining intelligent pressing and movement with reliable finishing. Burkardt also featured in Klopp’s first Germany squad, although his international opportunities proved less rewarding.

In GGFN‘s assessment of Germany’s opening Nations League performances, the striker received a C- grade after struggling to influence the defeat against Greece during a brief substitute appearance. Hütter revealed that he had already spoken with Burkardt following Thursday’s announcement.

“Of course I’m happy that Younes has been nominated,” Hütter continued. “On the other hand, I think it’s a shame that Johnny’s not there. Of course, that’s not part of my area of competency. I have no influence over that. I spoke with Johnny earlier today and, of course, it’s normal to react with disappointment when one is not selected. But I don’t have the impression that Johnny is hanging his head.”

The omission comes despite Burkardt continuing to compete with Ebnoutalib for a starting place in Frankfurt’s attack. Klopp has instead selected Ebnoutalib, returning Werder Bremen striker Niclas Füllkrug, and Arsenal’s Kai Havertz as his three nominal forwards for the upcoming internationals.

Frankfurt goalkeeper Noah Atubolu is another player who has failed to retain his place following Klopp’s initial 44-man selection. The 24-year-old, who joined Eintracht from SC Freiburg during the summer, was included in the second group of players assembled for Germany’s previous international window.

Atubolu has nevertheless continued to produce encouraging performances at club level. The goalkeeper played an important role in Frankfurt’s September draw against his former club Freiburg and subsequently contributed several decisive saves during the recent Bundesliga victory over Hamburg.

Klopp has opted for Marc-André ter Stegen, Alexander Nübel, and Jonas Urbig as his three goalkeepers for November. Hütter acknowledged that the reduction in squad size was always likely to leave some of his players disappointed.

“I haven’t spoken with Noah [Atubolu] yet,” Hütter concluded. “But the lads can obviously put two and two together. From an XXL squad nominated [for the last international break] to the next two Nations League fixtures [in November], things will obviously look different. Some are happy, some aren’t.”

Derby County dealt another injury blow with key forward set for six weeks out

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Derby County went another game without a win as they were torn apart by Norwich City 4-0 on Tuesday night. John Eustace is under serious pressure as DCFC host Bolton Wanderers, in what could be his last chance to turn things round at Pride Park.

The Rams have not been helped by injuries this season and they have been dealt yet another blow now. John Eustace has told the BBC that Sammie Szmodics is set for surgery on a knee injury that will see him out for six weeks.

The forward limped off in the defeat to Norwich City during the second-half. Szmodics had been playing through the injury but after aggravating it on Tuesday, surgery was the only option.

He has made a decent start to this season having returned to the club in the summer after an initial loan deal last term.

Losing Szmodics a significant blow for Derby County, particularly given their precarious position in the Championship. Eustace’s side sit 23rd with just seven points from 14 games, having recorded only one win all season.

Szmodics is joint-top scorer with three league goals, alongside Bobby Clark, and has also provided one assist. Eustace described the loss as “huge” after confirming Szmodics will undergo surgery and miss approximately six weeks, potentially ruling him out until mid-December.

DCFC already have several players unavailable. While six weeks might ordinarily be manageable, the Rams’ lack of goals, creativity and squad depth makes this a considerable setback as they attempt to escape the relegation zone.

Filip Bilbija looks the most likely direct replacement for Szmodics in the No.10 role, with Bobby Clark another strong option. Szmodics has predominantly featured as an attacking midfielder in Eustace’s preferred 4-2-3-1 formation, operating behind the striker with freedom to get forward.

Bilbija has already been tipped to fill that position against Bolton, allowing Clark to remain in the double pivot alongside Lewis Travis or Alex Mowatt. Clark is also joint-top scorer with three goals and has the versatility to play further forward if required.

Other alternatives include Rhian Brewster, who can operate centrally or out wide, while Mohammed Fuseini and Corey Blackett-Taylor offer further attacking options. Henrik Meister and Carlton Morris could also provide cover in a supporting role.

Ryan Hedges is another fitness doubt, adding to Derby’s injury concerns. Eustace has options, but none quite replicate Szmodics’ experience and goal threat in the No.10 position.

Allegiant Says MAX 7 Seat Cost ‘Loses Pace’

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Allegiant prefers the economics of the Boeing 737 MAX 8 over the smaller MAX 7 as its plans for premium seating change how it evaluates the two aircraft.

The MAX 7 originally accounted for the majority of Allegiant’s Boeing commitment. In January 2022, the airline announced an order for 50 aircraft, split between 30 MAX 7s and 20 high-density MAX 8-200s, with options for another 50 jets.

The agreement, signed in December 2021, was Allegiant’s first direct Boeing order and marked a shift for a carrier that had largely built its business around buying used aircraft. At the time of the announcement, Allegiant operated 108 Airbus A319s and A320s.

Boeing said the new jets would reduce fuel consumption by about 20% compared with the older Airbus aircraft.

Speaking during the carrier’s third-quarter earnings call Wednesday, President and Chief Financial Officer Robert Neal said the MAX 7 initially offered an attractive balance between the cost of operating each flight and the cost per seat.

“When we did the order, we really liked the MAX 7 because we were looking at both the MAX 8 and MAX 7 at very high density,” Neal said. “And so you’ve got a really nice trip cost seat cost mix with the MAX 7.”

That calculation has changed as Allegiant expands its extra-legroom Allegiant Extra product and prepares to introduce Allegiant First.

“But over time, as you’ve seen us roll out incremental premium offerings at the moment through Allegiant Extra and eventually with Allegiant First, the seat cost just sort of loses pace with the MAX 8,” Neal said. “And so we prefer the cost economics of the MAX 8 at this point.”

Allegiant began shifting its order toward the larger variant in September 2023. An amended agreement retained the 50 firm aircraft but increased the number of MAX 8-200s, reduced the number of MAX 7s and expanded the option pool to 80 aircraft.

The MAX 7 faced years of certification delays, including work to redesign its engine anti-ice system. The FAA approved the variant on Aug. 3, after requiring changes to that system, flight-control software, and cockpit alerts.

The ultra-low-cost carrier added to its MAX 8 commitments last month, exercising six options for delivery in 2028. That brought its firm Boeing order to 56 aircraft, including 22 delivered through Oct. 23, with 74 options remaining.

Retirees Reign Over Homebuying as First-Timers Remain Older Than Historical Norms

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

As affordability remains one of first-time buyers’ biggest hurdles in real estate, the face of homebuying remains squarely retirees and members of older generations who are repeat participants in the housing market.

The median age of a homebuyer now stands at 58, and there is “increased buying activity among those over 60,” according to the National Association of Realtors®’ (NAR) 2026 Profile of Home Buyers and Sellers. Repeat buyers—who have a median age of 62—accounted for 78% of all home purchases.

First-time buyers claim only 22% of housing market activity, and the younger generations remain locked out as the median age of a first-time buyer rests at 40—roughly the same as last year.

Jessica Lautz—NAR deputy chief economist and vice president of research—noted in a release that the market share for first-time buyers is about “half the historical norm of 40% seen before 2008” as “affordability remains (their) biggest hurdle.”

“Those saving on their own continue to face high rent, student debt, and a shortage of homes they can afford,” she continued.

The generational transfer and the “silver tsunami”

In Lautz’s view—as she states in an interview with RISMedia—one of the biggest takeaways from the report is the “growing share of all cash first-time homebuyers,” who she notes are “very different from your traditional first-time homebuyer.”

“Their median age is in their 50s, and compared to someone who is financing, whose median age is in their late 30s (38, according to the report),” she explains. “We’re seeing that there’s a growing share of inheritance that is playing a role for these first-time homebuyers. The idea of the silver tsunami, the generational transfer of wealth, it’s starting to happen, and we’re picking it up in the data.”

The “silver tsunami” refers to a potential wave of inventory released into the market due to a possible increase in older Americans selling their homes, something that housing economists have long debated, with more focus on the demographic recently as home sales slow.

Over the summer, Bright MLS Chief Economist Lisa Sturtevant broke down this potential phenomenon in another recent report investigating how much stock housing market observers should place in this “tsunami.”

Sturtevant defined the “silver tsunami” as mortgage-free 70-plus year old homeowners listing their homes. Currently, about one-in-five homeowners are 70 or older, while about 45% of homeowners are 60 years old, she reported.

However, Sturtevant found that this “tsunami” will be unevenly distributed throughout the country, as the majority of 70-plus homeowners are concentrated in relatively more affordable Midwest metro areas.

“Will demographics solve the homeownership affordability challenge? The answer is no, but the aging of the population will dramatically shift the housing market landscape in specific parts of the country,” said Sturtevant.

A recent Realtor.com® report corroborated Sturtevant’s idea that even though raw aggregate numbers show a tsunami (and a growing one at that), with a 33.7% increase in housing relinquishments by older Americans in the next decade, closer analysis reveals that the tsunami may only be a trickle in some markets.

“Supply and demand are geographically mismatched,” the report stated. “(O)ur research shows that the impact also varies sharply by home type: Family and large homes see relatively larger relief, while starter homes see comparatively little.”

Lautz explains that there is not yet a full tsunami in the market, but generational transfers of wealth are occurring, and they are changing who can buy a first home and when.

“We’re seeing that there’s a growing share of inheritance that is playing a role for these first-time homebuyers,” she continues. “Because people are living longer, the generation receiving that wealth is no longer young adults. Many are reaching middle age before they can buy their first home.”

Over the past few years there has been an increase in multigenerational living, with 14% of buyers purchasing a multigenerational home. The report lists the top reasons for these purchases as: taking care of aging parents (30%), cost savings (23%), children over the age of 18 never leaving home (19%) and children over the age of 18 moving back home (18%).

“I think we’re at a new normal where people need to pull finances in a different sort of way to be able to afford property,” explains Lautz.

Lautz also highlighted childcare costs as a large reason for multigenerational living, as it is moving up in the data.

“For families who do have kids under the age of 18, it’s increasingly playing a role in why people are choosing multigenerational settings because it’s an extra set of adult hands,” she says.

Lautz adds that with multigenerational living there is a pathway into homeownership forming for first-time buyers as “a way to actually encourage homeownership in the long term is to allow them to skip paying market value rent.”

The report cited Census data, which stated that the share of young adults (aged 25-34) who are living at home is elevated—up from 9% back in 1980 to 16.4% now. With this increase, more first-time buyers who have made this move are able to buy a home—up from 12% in 1993 to 21% now.

Homeowners are also remaining in their homes for longer than historical data. The median residency in a home before selling was 12 years in the report, an all-time high.

This is partially due to the “lock-in” effect in the market, where homeowners with lower rates are waiting longer periods to sell so as to not have to spend more money monthly.

“The golden handcuffs, the low interest rate mortgages, make it appealing to stay put for a longer period of time,” Lautz says. “We’re seeing home sellers who are successfully making those housing trades, holding on to homes for a longer period of time. That’s allowing them for their next purchase to put down a very large down payment or even an all cash purchase.”

As retirees lead the market, though, Lautz notes that the reasons people are moving have shifted.

The report noted that the reasons for moving have shifted away from necessities due to jobs or life events (falling from 25% of buyers in 2000 to only 5% in 2026), and toward moving to be closer to family and friends (rising from 6% in 2000 to 14% in 2026). This is directly due to retirees being more active in the market.

“Seeing these optional moves lead the housing market means that I think there’s a lot of people who are settling in and staying in place for longer periods of time too,” Lautz adds. “I think all of this is related, but it also means there’s that lack of inventory for first-time homebuyers that’s freeing up because of fewer housing trades as well.”

Despite all of the challenges agents have faced in the market and in the public perception as of late, NAR’s report had a positive note to share: agents remain essential guides to buyers and sellers navigating the market.

The report outlined that 90% of all homebuyers used an agent or a broker to assist on their purchase, which was the highest share recorded. In addition, 92% of home sellers used an agent or a broker, which was an all-time high. Only 5% of sales were for-sale-by-owner, which matched the all-time low.

“We know that this is a very unique housing market—everything is very local when it comes to the housing market today, and each unique transaction is going to have its own complications as well,” Lautz explains. “Having someone by your side to help you purchase that home and then sell that property, tell you how to fix up that home for sale, walk you through your unique transaction, I think is becoming increasingly important.”

Claudia Larsen is a senior editor for RISMedia.

The Acoustic Science of What Makes a Great Singer

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

A psychoacoustic investigation led by researchers at Keio University’s Neurosciences and Music Laboratory reveals the objective acoustic properties behind subjective vocal competition judging. Simulating a live classical singing competition in a concert hall, the researchers evaluated 22 classical vocalists performing the aria “Caro mio ben” before five expert judges while measuring core acoustic metrics.

Published in the Journal of Voice, the findings show that overall scores correlate strongly with perceived vibrato, resonance, expressiveness, and acoustic energy in the 2–4 kHz “singer’s formant” range, rather than raw loudness or intonation accuracy. The study also uncovered sex-specific judging criteria: faster vibrato penalized male vocalists, whereas wider vibrato elevated scores for female singers.

Source: Keio University / Journal of Voice

In classical opera and vocal competitions, expert adjudicators often render definitive judgments within seconds of a performer’s opening phrase. Yet, the pedagogical and aesthetic criteria underpinning these scores have remained steeped in metaphorical, subjective terminology, instructors and adjudicators routinely urge vocalists to produce a sound that is “warmer,” “more focused,” or “ringing.”

Because classical singers perform unamplified in massive concert halls, projecting over full symphonic orchestras requires an extraordinary coordination of respiratory mechanics, laryngeal posture, and vocal tract resonance. However, the lack of standardized, objective acoustic benchmarks creates ambiguity for developing vocalists seeking to understand competitive judging outcomes.

To demystify these artistic evaluations, a research team led by operatic soprano and vocal science researcher Assistant Professor Haruka Kondo, alongside Dr. Sotaro Kondoh and Associate Professor Shinya Fujii at Keio University’s Research Center for Music Science and Neurosciences and Music Laboratory in Japan, simulated a live vocal competition to identify the exact acoustic markers that drive expert appraisal.

Publishing in the Journal of Voice, the investigators mapped how physical acoustic parameters correspond to human perceptual judgments in a professional hall acoustic environment.

To replicate natural performance acoustics, the team recruited 22 classically trained vocalists (10 men and 12 women). Each singer performed the Italian art song “Caro mio ben” a cappella, standing on stage in a live concert hall without microphone amplification or instrumental accompaniment.

A panel of five professional vocal judges scored each performance on a 100-point holistic scale while independently grading six discrete vocal attributes on a 7-point scale: vibrato quality, resonance, timbre, diction clarity, intonation (pitch accuracy), and musical expressiveness.

Concurrently, the researchers extracted four primary acoustic features from the audio recordings:

Statistical modeling revealed that overall competition scores were not dictated by sheer vocal power. Raw loudness (LUFS) showed no significant correlation with judges’ rankings. Furthermore, diction clarity, intonation accuracy, and descriptive timbre ratings failed to predict higher placement.

Instead, judges’ overall marks were determined by perceived vibrato quality, vocal resonance, and artistic expressiveness.

Acoustically, this preference was confirmed by the singing power ratio: vocalists who concentrated acoustic energy in the 2–4 kHz frequency band consistently earned the highest marks across both male and female cohorts. This acoustic peak corresponds to the classical “singer’s formant,” an anatomical acoustic clustering achieved by lowering the larynx and widening the pharynx. The formant enables a human voice to produce acoustic energy that cuts through the dense accompaniment of an orchestra without vocal strain.

“Our study bridges the gap between artistic intuition and science and does not aim to replace the expert ear. It helps translate what experienced listeners hear into a shared, measurable language,” explains lead author Haruka Kondo.

When evaluating vibrato mechanics, the researchers uncovered an unexpected sex divergence in how acoustic parameters influenced judge preference:

These findings suggest that expert listeners possess distinct, internalized physiological archetypes for male versus female classical singing, penalizing male voices for perceived tremolo (fluttering) while rewarding female voices that achieve broad, resonant pitch oscillation.

By isolating the physical acoustic correlates of vocal excellence, the study offers concrete diagnostic tools for classical vocal training.

“Our results show that something as personal and artistic as ‘a beautiful voice’ can be partly understood through science,” says Kondo. “This could help students understand what their instructors mean when they say, ‘let it ring more’ or ‘your vibrato is too shallow.’ Students could then use these measurements to see how their voices change as they practice between lessons, which could help organizers design clearer scoring sheets and help singers understand their results.”

Beyond the rehearsal studio, the authors emphasize that integrating these acoustic metrics into automated vocal analysis tools and mobile training applications could provide emerging singers with objective, real-time feedback that reflects the trained perception of professional adjudicators.

What acoustic feature most strongly predicts high scores in classical singing competitions?

Prominence in the 2–4 kHz frequency range, known as the “singer’s formant,” is the strongest acoustic predictor of high scores. This acoustic resonance allows an unamplified voice to project clearly without relying on raw, fatiguing vocal loudness.

Did vocal loudness or pitch accuracy guarantee higher competition marks?

No. Standardized vocal loudness (LUFS) had no measurable association with higher scores. Similarly, diction clarity and pitch accuracy (intonation) showed no clear statistical link to winning marks, as baseline technical accuracy is presumed at expert levels, leaving resonance, vibrato, and expressiveness as the differentiating factors.

How do vibrato standards differ between male and female opera singers?

Adjudicators evaluate male and female vibrato along distinct acoustic axes. In male singers, a faster vibrato rate was penalized with lower marks, whereas in female singers, a wider vibrato extent (greater depth of pitch modulation) was rewarded with higher overall scores.

Funding information This work was supported by the Taikichiro Mori Memorial Research Fund of Keio University, the Keio University Doctorate Student Grant-in-Aid Program from the Ushioda Memorial Fund, and JST SPRING [grant number JPMJSP2123]; by the Ishii-Ishibashi Fund (Keio University Grant for Early Career Researchers), JST COI-NEXT [grant number JPMJPF2203], JST PRESTO [grant number JPMJPR23S9], and JSPS KAKENHI [grant numbers 24H02199, 23K25750 and 25K21813]; and by JSPS KAKENHI [grant number 24KJ1930].

Vocal Evaluation of Classical Singing in a Concert Hall: Perceptual and Acoustic Associations with Overall Scores in Male and Female Singers

In classical vocal competitions, judges reduce a performance to a single overall score, yet what that score reflects is unclear. Research has separately identified the perceptual attributes experts use and the acoustic features of trained singing. Few studies link these to expert evaluation, and those that do have used playback of sound-treated recordings and rarely tested for sex differences. Yet classical singing is judged live in halls, and male and female voices differ acoustically. We asked which attributes and acoustic features are associated with the overall score in a concert hall, and whether they differ by sex.

Twenty-two classical singers (10 males, 12 females) performed Caro mio ben a cappella in a concert hall. Five singer-instructors rated each performance on a 100-point overall score and 7-point scales for six vocal attributes. Singing power ratio (SPR), loudness units full scale (LUFS), vibrato rate (VR), and vibrato extent (VE) were computed from annotated vowel segments of close-microphone recordings. Linear mixed-effects models related the overall score to the ratings or acoustic features, with sex interactions and a random judge intercept.

Higher ratings of vibrato, resonance, and expressiveness were associated with higher overall scores, whereas timbre, diction, and intonation were not. Higher SPR, greater VE, and lower VR were associated with higher overall scores, whereas LUFS was not. Sex interacted with VE and VR but not with SPR or LUFS: the VE association was stronger in females, the VR association in males.

Overall scores were associated with perceived vibrato, resonance, and expressiveness, and with spectral prominence at 2–4 kHz. Vibrato associations were sex-dependent: a faster rate accompanied lower scores in males, a greater extent higher scores in females. These findings provide measurable correlates of expert evaluation under realistic conditions and suggest that vibrato may need to be interpreted with reference to singer sex.

Buick Electra GS Is a Sleek New Plug-In Hybrid Sedan for China

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Designed for the Chinese market, the Electra GS looks significantly different than the concept shown last year, with sharp, C-shaped LED headlights.

While there are reports of a new sedan on the horizon, Buick’s lineup in the United States currently only consists of SUVs. But over in China, Buick offers a healthy arsenal of sedans, from the compact Verano to the larger LaCrosse. Now Buick is about to introduce a new electrified sedan called the Electra GS, and the first images of the new car have been leaked via China’s Ministry of Industry and Information Technology (MIIT).

The MIIT periodically publishes information on upcoming automotive products, and the official filing is the first time we’ve seen the final production design for the Electra GS, which was first previewed by a concept in 2025. Confusingly, Buick already offers a sedan called the Electra L7 in China, sold with electric and range-extender plug-in hybrid powertrains, but the new Electra GS seems poised to be a less potent and more affordable option.

See all results for used Buick for sale near 07047

The new Electra GS is a bit smaller than the Electra L7, measuring 196.0 inches from nose to tail, just over two inches shy of the L7. The GS is also 1.5 inches narrower and has a 5.3-inch shorter wheelbase than the L7. Under the hood, the Electra GS will employ a plug-in hybrid setup that uses either a 97-hp naturally aspirated 1.5-liter inline-four or a turbocharged 1.5-liter making 154 hp. The gas engines are paired with an electric motor producing 221 horsepower, which feeds off a lithium iron phosphate battery.

Stylistically, the production Electra GS is a significant departure from the concept, with a more traditional sedan shape than the concept’s taller, hatchback look. The front end has thin headlights that wrap around a trim piece that stretches across the front and can be either body colored or finished in silver. The Electra GS has a fastback roofline and smooth surfacing, while the taillights span the width of the vehicle before curving inward.

Overall, the Electra GS looks smart and modern, and Buick will hope that the GS can help reverse its sales slide in China. The Electra GS won’t be coming to the U.S., but it could give a sense of Buick’s future styling direction that could appear on the rumored gas-powered sedan expected to arrive in the next couple of years.

He loves quirky and obscure autos, aiming to one day own something bizarre like a Nissan S-Cargo, and is an avid motorsports fan.

Kenya Barris Bets on Black With Latest Revolt Reality Series

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Known for his hit comedy series Black-ish and Grown-ish, Kenya Barris is making his way back into unscripted programming, joining Revolt to executive produce the sixth season of the Bet on Black reality series.

With Barris’ arrival, Bet on Black introduced a refreshed format when it premiered on Oct. 19, in which student founders from Historically Black Colleges and Universities (HBCUs) receive hands-on mentorship and share in a $500,000 growth fund, with the opportunity to grow their businesses while retaining 100% equity in their ventures.

This is Barris’ first project with Revolt since he was recently named vice chair of its creative studio, Revolt Labs. In addition, joining Bet on Black has given him the chance to reunite, for the first time since the conclusion of Black-ish, with actor Marsai Martin, who hosts the reality series.

Speaking to ADWEEK, Barris said he was attracted to the series because of the name, adding that the idea of promoting the next generation of entrepreneurs was amazing and that he was impressed by Revolt’s execution of the series, saying it was done “at a higher level than I even thought that it could be done.”

He said it was important to invest in the next generation of Black founders, especially those coming out of predominantly Black universities, so that they can be presented in a better light and not caught up in the sometimes negative and perverted assertions of Black culture.

Barris, who gained his reality TV experience working on America’s Next Top Model, was impressed by the founders’ ideas on Bet on Black, saying he believes their ingenuity and brilliance will better the world and make their big ideas sellable.

Backing all of this is Target, which has been part of Bet on Black’s success for all six seasons, since 2021. Target’s partnership with Revolt has directed about $2.69 million to Black founders and their businesses by providing access to capital, resources, and growth opportunities.

This season, the retail chain has invested $500,000 in the show’s growth fund. Beyond that, Target is supporting the show’s marketing through TV, digital, CTV, YouTube, social, on-site, and influencer channels.

Revolt CEO Detavio Samuels said that Target’s continued investment in Bet on Black signifies “the importance of building mission-driven and purpose-driven brands.”

He went on to say it’s not enough to build a great company that makes great products and generates profits: “You also have to help create the world that your audience wants to live in.”

Samuels says Revolt has been committed to uplifting the Black community and trying to solve some of its biggest problems, and Target’s partnership reflects an advertiser committed to doing that work with them.

Samuels regards Barris as exemplary among Black creators: “Beyond just a Black space, I would very easily say [Barris] is one of the most prolific minds out right now, creating.”

Making his time at Revolt even more rewarding for Barris is the chance to team up with Martin, who played Diane Johnson, the youngest daughter, on the hit ABC show.

Proud of how all the kids on Black-ish turned out, Barris singled out Martin, saying she was just “a spark of energy and a spark of light.” He said she has known how to handle herself, from the first day she stepped on the Black-ish set to now, when they reunited on Bet on Black.

“It was really special to me,” Barris said.

Mark Mwachiro is a TVNewser contributor who has been writing for the blog since March 2022. Based out of New York, Mark has also contributed to ADWEEK.

Real REMAX Q3 Revenue Skyrockets; Company ‘Offboards’ Over 1,000 Agents

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

After the Real Brokerage completed its acquisition of RE/MAX Holdings this past August, the third quarter earnings for the company revealed a significant shift in revenue, sparked by AI investments and slight drops in agent retention.

Tamir Poleg—Real REMAX chairman and CEO—started off the first earnings call for the combined companies, and expressed his confidence in “delivering strong growth in adjusted EBITDA” even if the housing market “gets worse.”

He also acknowledged the increases in agent offboarding. The Q3 earnings release stated that 500 agents had been let go in October alone, with the total REMAX agent count in the U.S. and Canada reportedly down 3% from the previous year at 72,118.

“The housing market has been operating near trough levels for the past four years,” Poleg added. “The recent increase in mortgage rates has further delayed the recovery that many, myself included, had anticipated. Beginning in September, we saw an uptick in the number of agents leaving the industry altogether. Real REMAX Group was not immune. Over the past two months, we’ve proactively offboarded more than 1,000 agents, many of whom allowed their real estate licenses to expire or left the industry.”

Real REMAX Group President Jenna Rozenblat echoed Poleg’s sentiment and noted that personnel other than agents were let go throughout the third quarter.

“We’ve reduced our combined headcount by more than 14%, which translates to over 150 positions across the organization while simplifying management structures and consolidating overlapping functions,” she stated.

While the series of layoffs and offboarding can be a cause for concern for some agents, Rozenblat emphasized the reason why restructuring is taking place, and mentioned the importance of AI technology.

“We don’t expect an immediate reversal in agent attrition in a franchise business, but our priority is to improve franchise owner economics and help them deliver a more competitive value proposition to agents and the clients they serve,” she continued. “Technology is an important part of that effort. We have already begun alpha testing Real’s proprietary reason software and AI capabilities for a phase rollout to the broader REMAX network.”

Poleg also briefly addressed a question on Open Real Estate, the initiative promising a new platform for listing distribution.

Poleg said Real REMAX believed strongly in its principles of “a more open, transparent and competitive approach to how real estate data is distributed,” but declined to comment on “specific products, economics or revenue opportunities” stemming from Open Real Estate.

On the point of AI, October’s introduction of Leo Pro—Real REMAX’s AI platform for agents and brokerages—could have instigated the large offboarding process. Poleg pointed out how Leo Pro can perform the functions “that traditionally required hiring additional staff, paying for multiple software subscriptions or spending hundreds of hours doing the work yourself.”

“The main usage of Leo Pro we’re seeing is creating marketing materials, social media posts and working their CRMs. It’s mostly nurturing their leads, and we’re seeing great conversions for our agents in terms of scheduling appointments for them and listing agreements,” Poleg stated.

Poleg also claimed that “historically, there has been a lot of disappointment with what REMAX has been offering their agents and brokers” in terms of tech, and that he has seen a lot of excitement from franchise owners.

“The fact that we have mature technology that is serving the Real side on the brokerage side gives them a lot of confidence that this is proven technology that can help them run better businesses,” Poleg said.

The data for the Q3 earnings call predates the release of Leo Pro.

Overall, the Real REMAX Group’s revenue increased 28% on a yearly basis, clocking in at nearly $728 million for Q3. The Q3 revenue for 2025 was $568.5 million.

The jump in revenue can mostly be attributed to the acquisition, as the release also notes that assuming the acquisition had taken place on Jan. 1, 2025 (a pro forma basis). It’s estimated that the combined revenue had increased by 20% instead to $770 million, compared to the prior year’s $641.8 million.

“On a pro forma basis, combined revenue increased 20% year-over-year to approximately $770 million compared with a 2% decline in industry existing-home sales transactions in the quarter,” Real REMAX CFO Ravi Jani said. “Within our brokerage business, Real continued to gain market share, while our franchise business continues to see attrition in North America.”

As for profitability, the company’s operating loss clocked in at $72 million for the third quarter, a significant increase from last year’s loss of $0.5 million. Jani attributed the growth in operating loss to the $52.3 million in acquisition-related expenses, and another $26.4 million in accelerated stock-based compensation related to employee terminations following the acquisition.

Net loss also grew dramatically year-over-year, at $67.2 million—or $2.25 per diluted share—for Q3. Last year’s Q3 net losses were estimated to be $0.3 million, or only $0.02 per diluted share.

Despite significant growth in losses and expenses—primarily caused by the acquisition—Real REMAX’s adjusted EBITDA increased 66% to $33.8 million in Q3, compared to $20.4 million the same period last year. The adjusted EBITDA margin also increased slightly by 4.6%, whereas last year’s margin was 3.6%.

For the final quarter of 2026, Jani expects revenue to fall, while EBITDA maintains itself in the mid-$30 million range.

“For the fourth quarter of 2026, we’re expecting continued pressure on housing activity, including declines in transactions per agent at Real and a year-over-year decline in REMAX’s North American agent count,” he concluded. “Against that backdrop, we expect fourth quarter revenue of $575 million to $625 million and adjusted EBITDA of $32 million to $38 million.”

Leo Pro may take center stage in the company’s Q4 earnings call and throughout 2027, as Poleg mentioned that plans to roll out the AI platform for the entire REMAX network are underway.

“Within a few weeks, we want to roll out Leo Pro for the entire REMAX network. Our operating platform for the broker/owners will be ready at some point in Q1 2027,” Poleg added. “I think that once that’s in place, we will be able to help a lot of the broker/owners with some of their struggles around profitability, freeing up time to focus on agent attraction and teaching their agents on how to sell more.”

Prior to joining RISMedia, Desirae wrote stories geared toward policy issues for the Connecticut Mirror. She also worked as a staff writer for The Daily Campus, UConn's student-run newspaper.

What is commotio cordis? The rare condition that may have killed Jesús de Miguel

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

It can cause sudden cardiac arrest after a blow to the chest

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

A recent incident during a Spanish Segunda División match between CD Tenerife and FC Andorra resulted in the tragic death of Tenerife player Jesús de Miguel. Although no official statement has confirmed the cause of his death, some reports suggest it could have been commotio cordis.

This is a rare but well-known medical condition that can cause sudden cardiac arrest after a blow to the chest.

Commotio cordis means “agitation of the heart”.

It was first described by doctors in the 1850s. In 1932, researchers in Germany identified three factors that help explain how it happens: the type of impact, its speed and where it strikes the chest. A sudden, sharp blow is particularly dangerous.

The heart sits inside the chest, protected by the ribs and the sternum, the flat bone at the front of the chest. Its lower tip points towards the left side of the body.

Unlike other muscles, the heart contains specialised muscle cells that generate their own electrical signals, allowing it to beat without conscious effort. It contracts thousands of times a day to keep blood circulating around the body, adjusting its activity to meet changing demands.

Heart muscle also does not tire in the same way as the muscles we use to walk, run or lift things. It can keep working throughout our lives because it relaxes between beats, giving itself time to recover.

This relaxation depends on a carefully timed electrical process. During part of each heartbeat, the heart’s cells reset their electrical charge so they are ready to contract again. This stage is known as the T-wave and can be seen on an electrocardiogram (ECG), which records the heart’s electrical activity.

During this brief period, the cells are normally less likely to respond to another electrical signal. But a powerful blow to the chest can send energy through the body’s tissues and disturb the electrical activity of the heart.

Adam Taylor is a Professor of Anatomy at Lancaster University.

This article is republished from The Conversation under a Creative Commons licence. Read the original article.

For commotio cordis to occur, the impact must happen at a very specific moment in the heartbeat. The vulnerable part of the T-wave lasts for roughly 30 milliseconds – just three hundredths of a second. A blow during this tiny window can interfere with the heart’s normal electrical rhythm.

The result can be ventricular fibrillation, one of the most serious heart rhythm disorders. Instead of contracting in a coordinated way to pump blood around the body, the heart’s lower chambers, called the ventricles, begin to quiver chaotically.

Blood can no longer circulate effectively to vital organs, including the brain. This causes cardiac arrest, in which the heart stops pumping blood properly and the person loses consciousness.

One unusual feature of commotio cordis is that the heart and surrounding tissues can appear completely normal afterwards.

There may be no structural damage to the heart muscle, coronary arteries, ribs or sternum. This distinguishes it from cardiac arrest caused by injuries that physically damage the heart or chest.

The condition is rare but life-threatening. It is most commonly reported in children and teenagers aged four to 18. Most recorded cases involve males, who account for 94% of sporting cases and 93% of non-sporting cases.

Younger people may be more vulnerable because their chest bones and muscles are still developing. Their chest may offer less protection against the force of an impact reaching the heart. Women account for a larger proportion of non-sporting cases, at around 13%.

Football has seen deaths linked to commotio cordis before. A study examining sudden deaths in football worldwide between 2014 and 2018 recorded 617 player deaths.

Among players aged 35 and younger who died suddenly while playing, 9% had, or were thought likely to have had, commotio cordis. In 11 cases, the impact came from a football hitting the chest. In three, it came from an opponent’s fist or elbow.

The condition has been reported more frequently in other contact sports. Baseball is often cited as the sport with the highest number of cases. Ice hockey, American football and lacrosse have also recorded more deaths than football.

One of the best-known recent cases involved NFL player Damar Hamlin, who suffered commotio cordis during a televised match. Medical staff responded immediately and saved his life. He returned to playing nine months after his cardiac arrest.

The speed of treatment is crucial to survival. Research suggests that when emergency treatment begins within three minutes, survival is more than 70%. If treatment takes three to five minutes, the chances fall to around 50%. After five minutes, they drop to approximately 25%.

Outside sport, commotio cordis can happen during assaults or road traffic collisions. Survival in these situations depends partly on whether someone nearby recognises what has happened, starts emergency treatment and has access to equipment such as a defibrillator.

Researchers believe commotio cordis deaths are probably underreported. This may be because it can be difficult to distinguish the condition from other causes of cardiac arrest, particularly when nobody witnesses the original impact.

Greater awareness of commotio cordis beyond cardiology, alongside the development of a national registry, is helping researchers understand the condition better. These efforts, combined with faster emergency treatment and improved access to defibrillators, could increase the chances of survival for people who experience it.

Food delivery startup Wonder files for IPO as it explores its next big bite

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Food delivery startup Wonder is moving closer to becoming a publicly traded company after confidentially filing for an initial public offering (IPO). The company announced the filing on Thursday, marking a major milestone following years of fundraising, acquisitions, and expansion.

Wonder has yet to reveal how many shares it plans to make available or the expected price range.

Founded in 2018 by entrepreneur Marc Lore, Wonder has evolved from a food delivery concept into a broader food technology business. Its model goes beyond traditional delivery apps, with the company operating food hall-style locations where customers can order meals from multiple restaurant brands through a single platform. The company handles everything from meal preparation to delivery, pickup, and dine-in service.

Lore, who previously founded Jet.com and Diapers.com, has made no secret of his plans to take Wonder public. Those ambitions gained further attention in July, when the company announced it had raised more than $650 million in a funding round that valued it at around $9 billion. Investors include Accel, GV, AllianceBernstein, and ARK Invest.

Meanwhile, Wonder has been busy expanding its footprint and strengthening its business through acquisitions. The company has outlined ambitious growth plans, including a major push into Texas, where it aims to open more than 100 locations by the end of 2027.

Additionally, Wonder has acquired meal-kit company Blue Apron, restaurant delivery platform Grubhub, media company Tastemade, and kitchen robotics company Spyce. The latter acquisition gives Wonder access to automation technology that could help streamline food preparation and improve how food is served.

Wonder is also branching out with Wonder Create, its platform focused on letting food entrepreneurs and social media influencers develop new food concepts, restaurant brands, and menu items. The platform leverages AI to design and launch restaurant brands in less than a minute.

Another notable move is its $425 million partnership with DoorDash to expand its campus dining business, which operates at 450 colleges and universities and other locations.

Despite its growth, a move into the public markets will bring new challenges. Wonder is operating in an increasingly competitive food delivery landscape, going up against established names such as DoorDash and Uber Eats.

A Mighty Wind Blew Away Gas and Made Early Galaxies Brighter

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Only a few hundred million years after the Big Bang, during the Cosmic Dawn, bright galaxies populated the Universe in numbers higher than expected. The JWST showed that these galaxies formed very dense, compact clusters of stars. Why their star formation was concentrated in such dense clusters is a puzzle, as is the brightness of their host galaxies.

A pair of researchers may have figured it out. Their work is titled "LyÎ± Radiation Pressure in Dense Star Clusters: Implications for Star Formation and Winds at Cosmic Dawn," and it's published in The Astrophysical Journal. The authors are Shyam H. Menon and Aaron Smith. Lead author Menon is a research fellow at the Flatiron Instituteâs Center for Computational Astrophysics, and co-author Smith is an assistant professor of physics at the University of Texas at Dallas.

The Lyman-Alpha (LyÎ±) radiation in the paper's title is a very important factor in astrophysics. It's produced when an electron in a hydrogen atom transitions from excited to ground state. Since hydrogen is by far the most abundant element in the Universe, LyÎ± is a fundamental way to probe the Universe's earliest galaxies, including the interstellar medium (ISM) and the intergalactic medium (IGM).

"Observations with the JWST in lensed fields have revealed that galaxies at cosmic dawn may concentrate their star formation in highly dense, compact, star clusters," the authors write. "The high columns and low metallicities encountered in their birth environments suggest that LyÎ± radiation pressure may be crucial to their formation and evolution."

These panels show some of the bright and dense star clusters the JWST found in the early Universe, only about 460 million years after the Big. The panel on the right shows their location in SPT0615-JD1, a strongly-lensed galaxy. Image Credit: ESA/Webb, NASA & CSA, L. Bradley (STScI), A. Adamo (Stockholm University) and the Cosmic Spring collaboration

âOur results show Lyman-alpha radiation can push low-density gas and dust away really, really fast,â lead author Menon said. âIt can sort of clear the âfog,â allowing us to see stars in the early universe much more clearly than we would otherwise.â

LyÎ±'s utility as a diagnostic tool also explains its powerful ability to push dust and gas away so quickly. LyÎ± radiation is different than others. Typically, when an atom absorbs energy, it re-emits it at a different wavelength. But hydrogen atoms absorb LyÎ± and then re-emit it again almost instantaneously, where it interacts with more hydrogen. This absorption-emission cycle makes it a powerful diagnostic tool, and it also gives LyÎ± the ability to bully hydrogen gas.

Astrophysicists knew this, but understanding its effect on a large scale was difficult.

âWeâve known for a very long time that Lyman-alpha radiation could be important because it carries a lot of force,â Menon says. âBut weâve never actually been able to estimate how important it is in a realistic environment because of how challenging it is to model.â

Researchers have tried to incorporate it into models, but Lyman-alpha resists those attempts. Some haven't even tried to include it. "The oversight is related to its high dimensionality and complexity, as well as the inherently demanding computational requirements for modeling it self-consistently," the authors write.

The reason it's difficult to model is precisely because of its repeating cycle of absorption and emission. But difficult isn't synonymous with impossible.

Menon and Smith didn't try to simulate it. Instead, they took snapshots from existing simulations and got busy calculating the force necessary to explain the snapshots. "In this study, we address this question by postprocessing snapshots from radiation-hydrodynamic simulations of dense star-cluster-forming clouds with a range of dust abundances using the COLT Monte Carlo code," they explain. COLT stands for Cosmic Lyman-alpha Transfer. Study co-author Aaron Smith is COLT's original creator, and he wrote it to "simulate the production and escape of LyÎ± from hydrodynamical simulations," according to the COLT website.

In short, they examined simulation snapshots of star-forming regions in the early Universe, and calculated the amount of LyÎ± necessary to explain what they saw.

These are multiple views of a 2.5-million-year simulation visualizing a gas cloud as it is cleared away and the formation of stars. From left to right, the views show the gas density, ionization and temperature. Credit: Shyam Menon (Flatiron Institute) and Aaron Smith (UT Dallas).

The results showed that Lyman-alpha radiation wasn't strong enough to blow away the gas in the dense star-forming regions found by the JWST. The gravity from the dense gas in those filaments was too strong for Lyman-alpha to overcome. But beyond those dense filaments, it was powerful enough to blow away the more diffuse gas and the dust.

This graphic shows how Lyman-Alpha was able to blow away gas and dust that obscured early galaxies and their dense star clusters. Image Credit: Lucy Reading-Ikkanda/Simons Foundation

The authors outline two simple conclusions in their paper: "LyÎ± radiation pressure is unlikely to preclude efficient star formation," and "LyÎ± radiation pressure can drive dynamically significant winds once star formation saturates."

"Therefore, our results suggest that the SFE is likely to remain high in the dense star clusters forming at high-z. On the other hand, a significant fraction of the volume-filling gas can be ejected by LyÎ± radiation pressure, suggesting implications for the visibility of light from the cluster, ionizing photon escape, and launching outflows from the galaxy," the researchers write in their paper.

So once star formation had proceeded far enough in these dense clusters, Lyman-alpha made them visible by blowing away the rest of the gas.

âWhen the density is low enough, the Lyman-alpha radiation can push the gas to really high speeds, launching explosive winds that can shoot material clear out of the galaxy,â Menon says. "That cleared away the gas obscuring the dense clusters, making them visible."

Evan Gough is a science-loving guy with no formal education who loves Earth, forests, hiking, and heavy music. He's guided by Carl Sagan's quote: "Understanding is a kind of ecstasy."

The Glimchers Can’t Keep Up the Pace

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The mega gallery is struggling, Ebony L. Haynes joins Marian Goodman Gallery, longtime Manifesta director steps down, and more in Art Movements.

Art Movements, published every Thursday afternoon, is a roundup of must-know news, appointments, awards, and other happenings in today’s chaotic art world.

The beleaguered Pace Gallery is in the news again, this time after its United Kingdom tax filings revealed that it lost $5.4 million last year and has a $19.2 million debt-for-equity swap in the works with its US parent company. The filings also outlined plans made in 2025 to cut costs and reduce full-time positions.

The reports come in the wake of an abrupt downsizing over the summer, when the gallery laid off 50 workers and slashed 50 artists from its roster in one fell swoop. (Multiple Pace employees told Hyperallergic that they learned of the cuts only when the New York Times broke the story in early June; one artist said the gallery demonstrated "a callous breach of trust.") Additionally, Pace closed its Hong Kong gallery last October after 12 years and is still looking for a smaller space for its London outpost, which put its 8,600-square-foot location on the rental market in June.

Nine months after Marian Goodman's passing at age 97, her namesake gallery has appointed Ebony L. Haynes as its inaugural senior director and global head of curatorial affairs. Haynes steps into the role after six years at David Zwirner, where she founded and led 52 Walker as a space run entirely by Black staffers. Under her leadership, the Tribeca gallery exhibited work by artists including Arthur Jafa, Cauleen Smith, Pope.L, and Gordon Matta-Clark. Marian Goodman Gallery also announced Rose Lord and Junette Teng, two of its longtime partners, as co-presidents.

It has indeed been a beautiful week in the neighborhood. Brad Lander spent Halloween in a house costume made by his daughter, Rose, days before securing New York District 10’s House seat in the midterm elections on Tuesday; Queens-based organizer and artist Claire Valdez also won her race, becoming the first Native person to represent the state in Congress.

As one commenter observed, you gotta dress for the job you want!

Real-world assets value of US investment behemoth Franklin Templeton on Stellar (XLM) chain officially hit a $1.1 billion milestone as net capital inflow into tokenized money market fund BENJI totaled $200 million.

RWA Foundation's report, based on Token Terminal data, now signals an infrastructure-scale gap in the volume of institutional funds between Stellar and XRP Ledger, two blockchain techs that share the same origin thanks to Jed McCaleb. Stellar became for Franklin Templeton the second network in terms of significance after BNB Chain, surpassing even Ethereum.

Almost all RWA liquidity inside Stellar is shared between four of the biggest platforms. The undisputed leader remains Spiko, holding about half of the market there with $2 billion in tokenized funds. Second comes Franklin with its flagship fund, while the BlackRock-backed Ondo and Realiz split the remainder, commanding about half a billion dollars each.

The presence of Franklin Templeton on the XRP Ledger is still limited to just the sgBENJI fund worth $200 million. For Stellar it's not only 10-fold dominance in unique RWA-tokens holders, but also hundreds of millions in monthly volume of asset distribution against just $22 million for XRPL.

Sudden overnight jump worth $200 million by BENJI on Stellar managed to amaze even the market veterans. Representatives of RWA-platform ZeusRWA confirmed that the liquidity injection came at the buzzer:

«Franklin Templeton decided to add another hundred million just as we were about to publish…», researchers noted in comments to the report release.

The most striking demonstration of the gap between Stellar and XRPL is in the stablecoin segment. Amid similar numbers in how many of them are on both of the chains, 'digital dollars' on the XLM-linked chain are more used in commercial payments where their monthly transit volume hit $20.48 billion. On XRPL the same metric is at $9 million, which proves that they are simply held on balance without real usage in the ecosystem's payment tunnels.

The spike in Stellar dynamics in the second half of 2026 is backed by successful integrations inside the industry. Chainlink's price oracle has been locked in, while a key long-term factor is guaranteed by a joint venture with the US central repository DTCC which is expected to be the direct release of traditional financial vehicles into on-chain space by the first half of 2027.

Why Unai Emery is right to stay at Aston Villa – and reject Man United

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Emery is on the verge of signing a new contract with Aston Villa beyond 2029, which feels a pointed message ahead of Sunday’s trip to Old Trafford and Manchester United

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

It was the kind of thinking that used to govern most of football. A few years ago – although it could really have been many examples from the last 50 – a promising manager was being pursued by one of the game’s wealthiest clubs. He had built up a fine team with an upwardly mobile club, and it looked like they could be on the brink of something special… but that one thought wouldn’t go away.

“These opportunities don’t always come along, so you have to seize them.”

The manager ended up taking it, but not quite seizing it. He didn’t perform to the level required, albeit amid a lot of debate around the circumstances he walked into. His career has never reached the same heights, but the key point is that its trajectory was, in that moment of acceptance, drastically changed.

The old thinking was wrong. That job just wasn’t right for him.

He’s not the only one. There are a few examples, from Graham Potter to Julen Lopetegui, and there’s much more to it than just “backing yourself”.

Unai Emery is at a very different point of his own career, having experienced historic Europa League success  – with three different clubs – over 22 years, but it’s impossible not to wonder whether any of this weighed on his own thinking as he now discusses a new deal with Aston Villa.

The Basque will be all too aware of the noise around Manchester United’s interest, and the latest developments – including Villa’s very ‘LinkedIn’ post about "long-term strategic direction" – all felt very pointed ahead of Sunday’s trip to Old Trafford. Many in football even believe that one reason his club resorted to the absurdity of last week’s legal threat against Jamie Carragher was aggravation about their manager consistently being linked to “a bigger club”.

This, it should be acknowledged, is still Manchester United. It’s Sir Alex Ferguson, Sir Matt Busby and his babes, and so much glory and legacy. Despite all the current problems at Old Trafford, there is a history and consequently inspiration for the future; of what you might be able to do with that.

Others in football believe Emery might previously have been open to joining such a club, primarily because of the will to finally win a major league and the Champions League.

That isn’t to say either are indefinitely impossible at Villa given the club’s rapid recent growth, but their own fans have been frustrated over regulatory constraints.

And yet such strands lead into the nub of this, where this new thinking is warranted.

Emery’s record above Europa League level might raise considerable questions over whether he would suit a club of United’s scale, and those at Arsenal and Paris Saint-Germain have long insisted his outlook didn’t fit.

Far more relevant, however, might be that United just don’t suit Emery.

There are numerous persuasive arguments as to why he should absolutely sign that contract and stay at Villa.

None of them are to deepen these debates about club size, or even Emery’s own career.

It’s more that the 55-year-old is in the very rare modern position of having earned an almost ideal job.

At Villa, everything is built around him, in a way you just don’t really see at this level any more. The only comparison in the Premier League or any of Europe’s wealthiest clubs is at Arsenal, where Mikel Arteta was only given such authority after initially proving himself. Emery has almost complete control, and those circumstances have ensured everything is in tune. The work of a very good manager is consequently greatly amplified, so that Villa consistently over-perform.

That can even be seen in how they’ve responded to the great frustration of the summer. They’re already in far better shape than would have been expected. Emery might have had his Europa League winners ripped up, but it is already impressive how he has built a coherent unit. Sure, there are still issues and gaps that are difficult to fill amid constraints, but there’s a bite there. This is personified in Nicolas Jackson.

And, from that, and a relatively volatile Premier League, there is a real opportunity for top five again. That can bring further growth… but maybe more? He’s already had more at Villa than most managers will ever enjoy, since he is an adored club legend.

By contrast, links to United have been met with scepticism by tranches of the Old Trafford support. They are also fully aware that the actual problems are way above the manager. United are unlikely to give Emery the keys to the club, in the way Villa have done. Sir Jim Ratcliffe has already been insistent on building a modern sporting director culture, even if this iteration has so far failed.

Worse, a U-turn on that, to make someone like Emery manager, would actually just look even more indicative of dysfunction; that they’re going from extreme to extreme again. And that’s even before you get to the amount of rot that still needs to be cleared at Old Trafford. United are not yet close to being a club who just need a coach coming in and imposing his influence to get them winning. They require a patient rebuild, but the wait can derail coaches.

Put bluntly, if this comes down to a chance of winning the league or Champions League, there’s an argument that Emery specifically has a better opportunity at Villa, certainly in the medium-term. There would be so much more work to do at Old Trafford, with a lot more risk.

Maybe most pertinently, he would be giving up a very good thing, a very rare thing, that has the potential for so much more. And it’s that context that is also so key to such debates. It’s not just that the thinking has started to change. So has the reality. With more and more clubs looking to head coaches in wider infrastructures, the jobs are much more transitory. Such opportunities actually come along more frequently, but they’re far more contoured than before.

Better-run clubs than United are now geared in such a way that they only suit certain profiles, or certain ideologies. Tactical shifts can take time, and be painful, as currently being seen at Newcastle United. On Premier League pitches, the game is so systemised and optimised that the margins have become very thin. Hence wild swings in results when clubs aren’t in tune.

At Villa, it’s almost note-perfect for Emery. That isn’t something to be given up easily.

1968 Ford Mustang For Sale Is A Clean, Green V8 Cruiser Begging To Be Driven Daily

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

When you hear "green 1968 Ford Mustang," you likely picture one very specific car. You know the one: a verdant, tire-killing fastback driven by mercurial automotive and Hollywood legend Steve McQueen in the 1968 film "Bullitt." Today, a first-generation Ford Mustang Fastback similar to the one from the 58-year-old action-crime thriller could demand six figures, especially in that oh-so coveted color: Highland Green. For first-gen Mustang fans who can't afford to spend fixer-upper-home money on a car, there are alternatives. Alternatives like this green 1968 Ford Mustang.

At a glance and on paper, it ticks boxes. A V8 under the hood? Of course. That classic facelifted first-generation Mustang look? Thankfully, it's intact. Mostly. See, someone thought this one was deserving of a cool restomod treatment, and it got just that. Instead of being a numbers-matching carbureted survivor, this one benefits from a myriad of modern and aftermarket updates, like electronic fuel injection, electric fans, power locks, keyless entry, and Vintage Air air-conditioning and heat. This is a daily-driver-ready take on a classic. It's up for grabs, too. As of this writing, the restomod-ed Mustang is for sale just north of Dallas. Purists need not apply.

No, it's not finished in Highland Green. Not even close, really. The 1968 Ford Mustang restomod currently for sale by PC Classic Cars in Sherman, Texas is a much lighter hue. The headlights have been replaced with an integrated LED lamp-and-signal set and the tail lights are sequential like an aftermarket set you'd find on a much newer Mustang. It also rides on a set of staggered two-tone Halibrand-style alloy wheels that complement the paintwork, wrapped up in Nitto rubber. The builder also swapped in a Mustang II front end, complete with a sway bar and tubular control arms.

Under the hood, the '68 Mustang gets a familiar engine option: a 289-cubic-inch V8. It's far from stock, though. The parts list continues with a set of aluminum AFR cylinder heads, a Holley Sniper EFI setup, a Fi-Tech electronic ignition, an aftermarket serpentine system, an aluminum radiator in place of the tired old stock unit, and dual electric fans, to name a few things. The whole thing hooks up to a four-speed automatic gearbox with overdrive. Sorry, no manual here.

There are more modern touches beyond the engine bay, including power locks with keyless entry via an aftermarket fob, a stereo system with an amplifier and subwoofers, and that Vintage Air climate control. The restomod treatment continues inside the classic Mustang's cabin. It's a full brown leather TMI interior with black accents on the folding bucket seats and dash paneling. The brown leather even extends into the trunk.

It's not perfect. A glance at the driver's side door reveals a little misalignment. The car was once red. There are blemishes here and there. In short, this is more of a driver, less of a concours condition car meant to be kept out of the rain or prolonged direct sunlight. That doesn't mean the modified pony car loses the aesthetic charm that landed the 1968 model on the list of your favorite Ford Mustangs. Well, the fastback did, at least.

So just how much does PC Classic Cars want for this one? It's currently on sale for $46,950. That's quite a bit steeper than the average $25,310 price of 1968 Ford Mustang coupé sales over the last year. Then again, not every '68 Mustang on the market has had this much work done.

Get this: Its clock shows just 119 miles. Though, as you might expect, that's not exactly accurate. It's likely that this build's underpinnings have a much higher mileage. Frankly, a 1968 Ford Mustang with a 289-cubic-inch V8 and an true odometer reading anywhere near 119 would demand a much, much higher price. Still, with the modern tweaks already on this 58-year-old 'Stang, it seriously needs a driver to take it on as a daily and run that odometer up. After all, what would McQueen do? He'd almost certainly drive it — and so should the next owner.

AppLovin Is Back To Smashing Earnings, But Investors Are Slow To Buy

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

AppLovin is growing at a blistering pace, but Wall Street investors remain cautious about the business.

The mobile ad tech company earned just shy of $2.2 billion in Q3 this year, per the company’s earnings report after-hours on Wednesday. Of that, $1.44 billion fell straight to net income (which is to say, profit). That is an “industry-leading” profit margin of about 66%, said CFO Matthew Stumpf.

It’s the type of number ad tech companies haven’t seen since Rocket Fuel hit the market. Which, admittedly, isn’t such fantastic company, considering Rocket Fuel’s disastrous downfall. But that association – not with Rocket Fuel, but with third-party ad tech – is part of the challenge for AppLovin.

Co-Founder and CEO Adam Foroughi addressed the education and mind-shifting among media buyers (and, ahem, among its investors) that needs to happen for its consumer ad business to accelerate.

“People have to go, ‘This is a bigger ad tech company, that’s sort-of-independent ad tech without a first-party site, [and] bigger than anything we’ve ever seen before,’” Foroughi told investors.

There is a great deal of “headroom for growth,” as Foroughi put it multiple times throughout the call, when it comes to the consumer ad business. But filling that headroom is going to take a lot of time.

Right now, AppLovin’s consumer ads segment is growing fast – more than 100% year over year, Foroughi said, though revenue for that segment is not disclosed. That business was only formally launched out of beta last year, so the revenue is still relatively low. The business will compound over time, he said, as advertisers figure out the platform and run incrementality attribution studies to feel confident with the ROAS.

Foroughi pointed to the changes Apple is making to iOS and mobile web inventory as another reason for advertisers to get on board with AppLovin’s platform.

“A lot of what’s called, I guess, traditionally, ‘open web,’” Foroughi said, is actually just a cheap reach play. That mentality is not like AppLovin’s ad system, which tracks and values impressions through to the point of conversion, he said. A healthy platform with strong ROAS may very well see CPM increases, despite being antithetical to the cheap reach mindset. But the mobile gaming space is “much, much bigger” now, he added, and the web is becoming less trackable.

“We think a lot of those dollars will shift into the app space,” he said.

Then there’s AppLovin’s creative knowledge gap.

Advertisers in the consumer segment have mostly not purchased ads in mobile games before, nor are they familiar with the particulars of how AppLovin’s media platform works, according to Fouroughi.  Mobile game advertisers all use the platform already, and they have specific creative that, say, offers a mini-game or test gameplay to prompt a download. Ecommerce advertisers on social media can’t just port over their creatives.

For one thing, he said that social platforms are all about super-quick videos with a hook delivered in a second or two. But AppLovin’s gaming audience is watching a full-screen ad for 30-60 seconds. It practically never happens on a social feed.

“There’s a learning process here that has to happen so that the media buyers really understand the framework,” Foroughi said.

Eventually, agencies and advertisers will figure it out and approach AppLovin as an always-on platform with a standardized approach to creative.

“There will be playbooks for our platform,” he prophesied.

AppLovin laid out an ambitious marker on its earnings call.

“We believe this business can compound at roughly 30% annually over the long term,” Foroughi said of the ad tech business.

The current earnings report has ad revenue up by 55% and a profit margin that, as Foroughi put it, “is probably second to none.”

But 30% growth over the long term is bold, considering the multibillion-dollar scale of AppLovin’s ad business. And investors are still wary.

Some advertisers purportedly anticipate price hikes on AppLovin, since there are now gaming and consumer advertisers bidding for the same impressions, Stephen Ju of UBS Investment Bank noted.

Matthew Cost of Morgan Stanley wondered when the “law of large numbers” would catch up with AppLovin, and the company could no longer project 20%-30% growth. He also asked Foroughi whether the headroom he promised would come from CTV. Foroughi didn’t think AppLovin would see “accretive” numbers on CTV anytime soon. But there is a pilot program running right now for AppLovin CTV ads.

Shweta Khajuria, of Wolfe Research, put it most directly: “As an ad tech company for investors who are questioning the visibility of your business model and how much confidence you have in the durability of your top-line growth, is there something else incremental that you would consider sharing that would give more confidence in terms of the way your product works?”

For one thing, Foroughi said, AppLovin has delivered strong earnings that exceed investor expectations in 15 of the past 16 quarterly reports. No other large public tech company has its combination of scale, growth rate and profit margin.

Still, the consumer business is too nascent to offer confident predictive benchmarks around its growth or revenue totals, he said. “You sort of have to make your own guesses.”

AppLovin’s earnings can’t just be hand-waved away, nor is its margin vulnerable to any sudden collapse, as happened when Rocket Fuel disclosed a take rate above 60%.

AppLovin is an actual economic force in the mobile economy.

“That ad spend is creating GDP,” Foroughi said. “It’s creating new transactions that otherwise wouldn’t have been there.”
